The Liberals were greatly elated on Wednesday by the Pembroke result. A Welsh member of Par- liament who spent ten days in the constituency dur- ing the contest tells me that he attributes the mag- nitude of the victory, first to the exceptional strength of the Liberal candidate as an exponent of national feeling, and in the second place to the resentment of the agricultural electors against the inequitable operation of the Rating Act, following upon the result of the East Denbigh election. NOT THE ONLY ONFS.—A man once had 801110 ducks stolen, and found them at a neighbour's, whom he took before a magistrate. When asked how he knew the ducks were his, he answered that they were a rare breed. Said the magistrate, "These are not a peculiar breed; I have some like them in my own yard." "Very likely," said the plaintiff, " these are not the only ones of the sort I have had stolen." A RARE DEMl:MT.—During a horse fair a Quaker sold a horse to a dealer. When the dealer asked if the horse was a good puller, the Quaker remarked, "Friend, it would delight thee to see him pull." The dealer bought the house, and, trying him next day, found he would not pull an empty cart; so, calling on the Quaker, he said: "I thought you told me your horse was a goed puller?" "No " said the Quaker, "I told thee it would delight thee to see him pull. It would have delighted me, too, but I never had the pleasure." "THERE is one very dramatic situation in my new play. THEY FRIGHTENED THE lIosT.—A masked and fancy-dress ball was being held. At midnight two policemen called and said the company must separate. "Not at all," said the host, "This is a private hou=c%" " Very well, sir," said one of the policemen ; "here are my orders, and I must take the names of all who are present." "Do so," res- ponded the host. The police went to work and put down all the names, making everybody feel supremely uncomfortable. Directly they had done- so, one of them walked up to the table and poured himself out a glass of champagne. "What right have you to drink my wine?" roared the host. "I supposed, when I was invited," responded the policeman, " that refreshments were included"; and, amid a roar of laaghler, he showed his card. Both men were simply guests. THE football hero stood behind the sofa upon which sat the girl he loved. But he had lost his nerve. Three times the fatal question hovered upon his lips and three times he was downed before he had gained five yards. The beautiful girl slightly raised her head. " Harold," she mur- mured, "hadn't you better go round the end for a try?" As he acted npon the signal, and tackled her round the waist, it was clearly seen that the score would be a tie. OrruN DONE—Ho had just returned to America from a European trip, and was telling of his adven- tures. "And, above all," he said, "I actually had the distinguished honour of placing poker with a king." The man in the linen coat had listened in silence up to this point, but now his lip curled scornfully as he replied : "That's nothing. I once played with four kings." "Really?" "Sure. A LEAF FROM A MODERN NOVEL.—She took the sugar-bowl from the tray aid sweetened her tea slowly. Not that she did this deliberately or with any special consciousness of the tea. Eleanor was not a girl who cared particularly—at any rate, she never yet had cared particularly—whether her tea contained too much or too little F ge.r. She would have swallowed the tea even h..d she not done what she now did, and put the ngsr in it out of the sugar-bowl. But the tray being there, and the sugar-bowl, Eleanor did vidist she had been in the habit of doing ever since those dear old days. Perhaps the act brought lo3ck to her mind memories of countless similar acts. Certain it is that there was a thoughtful expression in her face on this °°e-laion- It was white sugar, she remembered afterwards; nor was there anything either in the tea, or in the sugar, or in the bowl to make this all linger in her memory long after she had completely forgotten other instances when she had taken the ougar-bowl from the tray and sweetened her tea 1110111 V.

'CAPITAL" CHAT.

Now that there is talk of organising a Polar bal- loon expedition next summer to ascertain the fate ot the missing aeronaut Andre, a special interest attaches to the opinions of MM. Henri Lachambre and Alexis Machuron, comparsons de voyage with the explorer in one ot his earlier ad- ventures. Those gentlemen, prefacing a narrative of their experienices which will be published by .illessrs. Constable and Co., take a strangely optimistic view of Andree's probable situation. "Has not Audree," they say, " already been highly favoured by chance and accident. Has he not already, in his career as an mronaut,escaped from dangerous situations in which many others, perhaps, would have perished." Let us hope, then, that his lucky star will not forsake him, and that fortune, which favours the brave, will bring him back to us victorious.

Lord Charles Beresford, who has given notice of an amendment to the Ad irees calling for the es- tablishment of a Royal residence in Ireland, ex- presses great indignation, as an Irishman, that this important question has been so long neglected. "The Queen (God bless her)has,"says Lord Charles, "reigned over this Empire for more than 60 years,' yet out of all that time she has spent only sixteen days in Ireland. That,of course, is not her Majesty's fault. The explanation is no doubt that oar gra- cious sovereign has been strongly advised by her Ministers. That they have tendered wrong advice von can see from the kindly and royal reception that was given to the Duke and Duchess of York. It is not a question of politics,thank goodness. No sensible Irishman thinks of associating the Royal family with politics,and Irishmen, and women too, would be glad,l am sure,to welcome the Queen as they have welcomed her children. It would be a splendid thing for both Ireland and England if some leading member of the Royal family would become a dweller in Ireland, if only for a short time every year, and I mean to do my best to bring such a happy consummation about."

Many indications of the popular restiveness in the United States at what is pretty generally re- I rarded as an intolerable situation with regard to Spain and Cuba might be given, but one prominent instance may suffice. At a religious service held in , the Academy Building the other day the preacher took up the burning question, and in vehement language denounced Senor Dupuy de Lome as a liar , and a hypocrite and accused Spain of acting with I insincerity both towards Cuba and this country. It ' was, he declared, high time for the Washington G m overn ent to act and make it its duty to prevent the atrocious murder of women and children being perpetrated by the Spaniards in Cuba. These words were entirely' to the taste of the congregation. They burst into enthusiastic cheers,and as these subsided such cries as "Kick De Lome out." " Let's fight," were shouted from various parts ot the hall.

I It would be interesting to know how much truth there is in Sir Wemyss Reid's statement that Mr. I Chamberlain meant to push his pro-British policy in the bend of the Niger, even to the extent of war with France, and how far this has only been frus- trated by the Queen's alleged intimation to the Prime Minister that she would never sign a declara- tion of war again. If the latter statement were true, its leaking out would be unfortunate, as it would only encourage the French colonial party, which now appears to have obtained complete con- trol of the French Government,to pursue an aggres- sive policy, and to trample upon existing treaty arrangements regarding the territory of the Niger. It would be completely destructive of Lord Salis- bury's position in the negotiations with the French Foreign Office, and also in the Far East. With a Royal resolution of this kind behind him, Lord Salisbury could not be anything else but a limp statesman, preyed upon by foreign Powers, and making " graceful concessions" all round, even where be had the most absolute right upon his aide. But how does the story of any such intimation by her Majesty to the Cabinet comport with the Chan- cellor of the Exchequer's recent militant outbreak?

Much as has been done for the solution of the London sewage problem, the subject is far from exhausted, and the series of experiments on which the London County Council has embarked will be looked forward to with wide interest. One consi- derable series has already been completed, and a second is about to be opened. The result so far has been satisfactory. Since the question of the dis- posal of sewage first arrested the attention of scientific men and public bodies, the knowledge of the origin and spread of disease has undergone the n.w development suggested by the study of bacte- riology, and the bacteriologist will, therefore, play an important part in the second of the series of ex- periments. An accomplished expert in this branch of investigation has been engaged from Dr. Klein's laboratory at St. Bartholomew's Hospital. In the meantime the experiments at the two sewage mit- tens will be carried on in Dr. Klein's laboratory until a separate laboratory cam be fitted up specially for the work required by the County Council.

The Jewish community in London has been en- gaged in considering what ought to be the salary of i a Jewish minister. There is, as n most churches,a great want of uniformity at present in the salaries received by Hebrew ministers. In the West-end the salaries range from as high as £l,OOO to as low as 2300; while in the East-end they are often of very scanty proportions.

Richard 11. created Ralph Nevill Earl of Westmoreland, and his fourth eon, Sir Richard Nevill, married the heiress of the Beauchamp, of Vv orcester, and became Baron Abergavenny. NOTES AND NEWS.

One result of enforced army service or conscription is that a Government has not to offer its soldiers induce- ments to enlist. The German government for instance, now furnishes its soldiers with only one meal a day. If they desire or need more, they have to get it at their own expense. The Press has started on a campaign to better this state of affairs, but thus far has met with no success. Among all other nations soldiers' food is distri- buted in a less niggardly manner. In the French army there are two meals a day, while Russia and England give their soldiers three. In certain Russian regiments even, there are annual cooking contests held under the auspices of a special commission. Handsome prizes are awarded for the hest " kettles."

• The Spanish Exchequer has been badly outwitted in a ease of treasure trove. A peasant of Santapouce, the site of Italica,the city which was founded ny Scipio and served as birthplace to Trajan, whilst digging in his gardea came across something hard, which preyed to be a big copper jar. With the aii of three friends he got it out, and found that it was full of gold e.oins.They numbered. it is said, fifteen hundred; but the truth will probably never be known, as the four confederates at once divided the plunder. presently some news of the windfall reached the ears of the authorities,for the place was Crown property, and one hundred and twenty-Gee coins were extracted from the Anders. These preyed to be specimens of issucs under most of the Ca:liars, from Nero to Marcus Aurelius, including a very rare example of Otho's brief reign. Those recovered are valued at 10,000 pesetas t£4oo),but the remainder it is fearesi,have already gone into the melting-pot.

The water torture, one of the forms of punishment Practised by the Spanish In quisition is even now in tile insome penitentiaries. It consists in confining the prisoner in a closet too small for him to stir. While he stansls, water, one drop A a time,is allowed to fall from a faucet on his head. It is said that few persons can endure this punishment for more than an hour.

The energy stored up in the Niagara Falls is says " Science Siftings," put at 6,000.000 hors.-power. An engineering expert has tried to give a hemely illustra- tion of vrhst this means. He says : Suppose that pumps are placed below the falls for pumping the water up &Rain to its former level. If s man exerted a force of about twenty pounds per stroke, and worked at a fair rate for eight hours a day, it would take about twenty times the population of Great Britain to pump the water back as fast as it is flowing over the falls. If 80,000.000 Of us were engaged in manual labour, the maximum I amount of work we could do could be done ten times over by the power now going to waste. All the work of labourers, all our actual exertions in digging, hammer- ing, lifting, climbing stairs, running sewing machines OP riding bicycles, probably does not represent one-hun- dredth part of this stupendous power.

CHAPTER IV.

"It is unfortunate—very exclaimed Mrs. Hughes, as her husband folded up a letter from his nephew, Dennis Vane, and carefully replaced it in his pocket. "I wish we could stop his coming. Hs is so impressionable; and, of course, we know nothing of the Trehemes, though we are forced to appear as if we were thoroughly acquainted with them, and since your chat with them yesterday we seem to have been thrown so much together. It is most annoying ! " Mr. Hughes acted discreetly in keeping his nephew's letter in his own possession; for it con- tained a postscript which ran: "I hear the two Trehernes have been left in your charge. This must be a great anxiety to you, and I am hurrying to Les Avants, to share the anxiety with you. Hums comes with me.- Don't tell aunt—don' tI "

It was decidedly against the good man's con- science to conceal this ominous news from his wife, and lie felt himself somewhat of a traitor when she observed in a very pointed manner: "Of course, we shall not introduce Dennis to the Trehernes. ,It would place us in a most awkward position if be paid them any attention. I wish we could prevent his coming; but even a telegram would not reach him now. The only thing to be done under the circumstances will be for you to take him for long excursions every day, and to keep him entirely out of their reach. Young men have such a silly .and unaccountable habit of being attracted by girls who are pretty and 'dress becomingly. But there are endless delightful excursions that you can take him, are there not, dear ? " Mrs. Hughes glanced round interrogatively at her husband.

"Yes—oh, yes!" he answered, feeling a still greater traitor. "But Dennis has his own ideas on enjoyment. Have you not noticed that?" "Yes, I have, and I am sorry to say that my confidence in Dennis has been sadly shaken lately. He seems to be growing frivolous; Iyhenever I expostulate with him on the unseemliness of his attire, or on similar subjects, he only smiles. Now, for instance, I do not approve of his wearing sailor hats—straw yards, as he calls them—and tan shoes on Sunday afternoons when he goes for a walk. I may be narrow-minded "—Mrs. Hughes said this as if it were only an empty formula—"but I do not believe that a conscientious Christian could dress in such a conspicuous style on the Sabbath. Of course, he is happily still young. Five years to come, when he is thirty, let us hope he will have a greater sense of the fitness of things." "You are probably right, my dear," concurred Mr. Hughes. He had net the heart to offer any contrary opinion. He was already depressed by the burden of the secret that Dennis -Was evidently acquainted with the Trehernes--a secret that he would have liked to share with his wife, but which he felt in honour bound not to mention to her. He sauntered to the window and looked out meditatively. A storm was beating up fast from the south, and the white glistening snow mountains stood silhouetted against the black clouds. There was a hushed feel- ing abroad, as nature prepared in silence for the coming storm. "I wish you would put down that needlework, Maria," he said, "and come and watch the storm rolling up the mountain side. It is a grand sight:" "Yes, I will in a moment, when I have finished retrimming my hat. I'm turning the bows," she continued in a communicative voice; "they were beginning to look so very shabby. There! I have finished now." She gave a last touch to the ribbons as she spoke, and then froceeded to try on the hat. "It looks quite new again!" she observed triumphantly; "does it not, 'William 7" "Quite," he concurred, his eyes still fixed on the coming storm. "I am sorry we cannot take tea with the Trehernes to-day, as Dennis is sure not to arrive before to-morrow, and I was beginning to look forward to enjoying a good cup of tea. Mr. Chimes tells me that Mrs. Treherne gives three-and- ninepence a pound for her tea. It ought to be good at that price, ought it not ? " " ft ought." There was something almost mechanical in the way Mr. Hughes invariably agreed with his wife in matters of small importance. After a moment's pause he added: "The storm will be upon us in a few seconds now; the clouds are rolling up the mountain side fast, screening the lake and valley from our view. There is something very strange to my mind in being above a storm." Mrs. Hughes, happy in the assurance that her hat was successfully renovated, hastened to the window, and proceeded to watch with energetic admiration the grand spectacle that the mountain side pre- sented. The rain was descending in torrents, transforming the mountain road into a shallow watercourse, and all around were massed dark leaden clouds. Swiftly the storm was passing on its course, and its full force was soon expended. In a few minutes the clouds dispersed, blue sky appeared, and brilliant sunshine burst forth. Just then the luncheon bell rang. "Let us Hu down to the salle-h-man.qcr at once," said Mrs. Hughes ; "watching the storm has made me feel quits hungry." In the hall they came upon two new arrivals, who had evidently- faced the full force of the storm, judging from their ,kenched appearance. They were nevertheless talking very clo;erfully, as if they regarded their reeeet experiewe as a joke. Mrs. Hughes glanced anxiously towards them, and immediately recognised one as her nephew I "Oh. Dennis. surely you were not out in that dreadful storm!" 'Om hastening towards him. commiseration uppermost in her voice. "Poor. dear boy :" He winced ; for at that identical moment G n end ol ine and Constance Treherno were passing through the hall, -but to his great relief they failed to recognise him. "Your clothes imist be soaked through." continued his aunt, with growing anxiety ; "go rut once and change them."

" f feel as if I should like to eltnnge my skin too. The one I wearing now is saturated," he answered with 11 laugh. "But let me introduce my friend, Eric Hume. you have often heard in. mention him. itunt."

Mrs. Hughes distinctly remembered having done so; :incl in consequence of his being a Solicitor had acquired a dislike to him. She mistrusted solicitors as a body.

"Yes, slie spra Then, as she ginAcsl vat his forlorn, drenched figure, t ing somewhat distantly. solicitude for his condition aroso in her mind. "You must he gime lig wet as my nephew," she added anxiously. "What could have induced you to walk up from Montreux on a stormy day like this? "

"Don't trouble about us, Mrs. Hughes," he answered in an amused voice; "a good drenching wou's hurt either of us." " .nould like to feel quite sure of that," she said with an ominous shake of the head. "But where is your luggage? " "It is following; it will be up some time to-day." Mrs. Hughes gazed at the two young men before her with consternation, then turned abruptly to her husband and said in a whisper: "William, it is absolutely necessary that you lend these boys some of your clothes." it is not right, or seemly that they should appear in clericals.' "Nor would our consciences allow us tq do so, aunt," interposed Dennis; "we should feel such awful frauds." "Then, mark my words, William "—Mrs. Hughes spoke with tragical impressiveness—"they will both have rheumatic fever, and probably die of heart disease."

"Sad endings to a Swiss tour," observed Dennis. "But I was about to suggest that we should retire to rest, and give our clothes an opportnnity of being dried." "An excellent suggestion I " said Mrs. Hughes, LO a voice of relief. "Really, Dennis, I did not think yon had so much sense." It was a gratify- ing remark, but Donnis only smiled ; it was not in his nature to take offence. "And. William "—hers her voice became mysteriously low—"in the left- hand corner of my bonnet-box you will find a small bottle of—of cordial. Go upstairs with Dennis and Mr. Mune, ring for some boiling water, lad give them .ench a dose." _

I expect they have cleared the salle-a-manger of the tables and chairs, and are having games for the children; later, of course, they will have dancing. I wish I could join them. It seems so stupid to remain moping in our rooms when all the world is enjoying itself. I really think I'll go and just look on." "It does seem absurd to shut ourselves up in our rooms," concurred Constance.

"It does! and I'm not going to do it! You can follow or not. Act as your conscience dictates." With•a laugh Gwendoline opened the door and disappeared down the corridor.

Around the door of the sale-it-manger a group of parents were clustered, evidently enjoying the spectacle of their children's happiness. A game of blind man's buff was in progress, and the children's merriment and happiness were amusing to witness. People of all ages were taking part in the game; the younger ones infinitely happier by reason of the presence of elders. An aide-de-camp of the Czar, a most dignified personage usually, had joined the game and was dodging the "blind man," a young Englishman, with the adroitness of an experienced tactician. Gwendoline immediately recognised the blind man. He was Dennis Vane; and her colour rose at the discovery. But she shelved no disposition to join the players. She stood well back amongst the crowd of spectators at the door, wondering if he would be very surprised to discover that she and Constance were staying at the hotel, or if he was already aware of it. But one little French boy, Henri Thierry, quickly espied her, and darting in her direction exclaimed: "Oh, Miss Treherne, you must come and play. It is such fun! The new English gentleman makes a capital blind man. Do come." The ring of entreaty and the child's pleading, merry face were more than Gwendoline could resist.

"I wanted him to catch you!" he exclaimed. The laughter of the children instantly sobered into a ripple of amusement, as all eyes centred on the "blind man" and his captive. Mrs. Hughes, who was ensconced in the salon, knitting in hand, gave a gasp of dismay as she glanced through the glass door communicating with the salle-a-mangen Her husband, who had been beguiled into taking a band at whist, looked nervously over his spectacles in the same direction. M y DEAR NELLnc. For those who can afford to buy hats for immediate wear and cast them off after a week or two's use, the most suitable thing to purchase now Is the feather toque. This is quite the head-gear of the moment. I can imagine nothing prettier than a toque entirely composed of ibis wings, now to be seen in Paris. The hat in question was of beret shape, and one of the ibis feathers, which stood upright at the side to give the necessary aigrette effect, surmounted a cluster of holly leaves and berries, which nestled against the hair. The effect was becoming in the extreme.

Draped toques of grey cloth are becoming very fashionable. They look very well draped with 'wreaths of violets shading from dark to light, and a couple of plumes. Under the toque at the left is placed a deep purple rosette. Some charming and essentially spring-like hats are • already being shown at some of the most fashion- able shops. One very pretty design I have seen Was a little hat of fancy shot straw, trimmed with velvet and with lilies-of-the-valley used in pro- fusion. Another one a little more elaborate was of •grass-green satin straw, with a large bow of green ribbon in the front, clasped with a brilliant paste ornament, and the rest of the trimming at the back, consisting of huge clusters of white and mauve lilac, and cache-peigne of rose-pink adjusted to rest on each side of the coils of hair. The colours sound rather daring, but the effect is admirable in reality. The era wn is entirely covered at the top with violet leaves, which looked very pretty. A third fiat, suitable for a girl of 17 or so, has the shape of green straw profusely trimmed with violets, • lilies-of-the-valley, and °hen() ribbon. A pretty bonnet for a young matron is made of violet satin straw, beautifully trimmed with lace, clusters of violets, and a sweeping black lophophore.

A coat and skirt costume are now the fashion

- and very booonting too do they look in blue serge or even in coloured tweed. They should be lined throughout. The coat should be in the Chester- field double-breasted or open-fronted styles. ?or out-of-doors the spring garments are enticing. The high collar is gradually subsiding; the jacket is for the morning toilette. The visiting costume requires the small tight jacket, rendered much more graceful than the bolero by a short basque. " As a gentle transition from fur to velvet we have the vest of friathers which blends the two. Delightful little vests 'for slender figures are made With ostrich feathers, black, white, or grey, and Well curled. :he sleeve naturally merges into a mere epaulette, formed to give passage to the inner sleeve of the vest or chemisette, and made of satin to " harmonise " but not to "match," and the corsage is finished with jewelled belt and buttons. The skirt can be of the same material as the vest, or a complete contrast. If the jackeb is in black feathers the skirt may be black satin, with buttons and waistbelt of steel ; if it is in peacock feuthers, a pretty efiect will be obtained with sapphite, arnet, or turquoise velvet.

Velvet coats are alvrt.ye senart,in the springosnd Ib4re suitable than cloth for dressy wear. An ex. oite model is in violet or a deep green velvet. OHN NOBL

WOMEN'S CHAT.

During the whole of her long life.in the midst of public business so voluminous and exacting, her Majesty has never entirely abandoned the pursuits which were the pleasure and relaxation of her earliest years. Mention has been made of her practice of music and of her in- structore,and here it may be noted that within the last fifteen years the Queen has suug with Signor Tosti,as at an earlier period she sang with Lablache and Mendel- ssohn. One of the chief of her Majesty's relaxations consisted in the use of burin, and the etching needle,and there is preserved at Windsor Castle in the Print Room, a care- fully worked set of proof etching, the greater number of which are incidental portraits of royal children. Among the most delightful perhaps,is that of the present Empress of Russia,as a tiny girl of three. In all the ex- tracts from the Queen's journals, which have from time to time been made public,it will have been noticed how constantly she mentions that she sketched the scenery of the places visited by her. The early instruction given by Weds% and supplemented by the hints occasionally given by SirE. Landseer,wae not in landscape drawing, which was taught by Mr. Lear in 1848 and 1847. Since that time the Queen has taken lessons from Mr. Leitch, and within the last twelve years also from Mr. Green. The Pope does not appear to have suffered in the least from the excitement, which the sixtieth anniversary of his Holiness's.first Mass, occasioned. He is said to bum the best of health and spirits. Attired in red, and wear- ing a gold chain, Leo XIII was carried the whole length of St. Peters (Rome) which was crowded to overflowing, en the shoulders of six footmen, while the ringing notes of silver trumpets reverberated threugh the building. When Mass was over, the Pope was borne back through the church wearing the tiara, and keeping his hand raised in the form of a continual blessing. The ceremony was one of impressive magnificence. It does not seem to be generally known, that the Pope has a collection of birds, of which he is very fond, and a collection of pelicans, ostriches, and fawns, which also receive his frequent attention. He has a vineyard too, and the care of this and hie gardens,forms his chief diversion. In the latter, though they are not en "gay and prim" as British gardens, are many things of beauty. Lady Sybil and Lady Margaret Primrose, who "coma out" this season, are the two eldest of Lord Rosebery's four children, the younger ones being boys. Lady Sybil Myra Primrose, who is named after two of Lord Bea- consfield's heroines, is over eighteen, and her sister was seventeen last month. Now that he has two "grown up" daughters' it is understood that Lord Rosebery will entertain much more freely than formerly, and will in consequence be much oftener seen at the crushes and dinner parties of well-knowe members of society. It is not improbable that this may induce the ex-Premier to again take up an active political life as a relief to the eomewhat monotonous routine of society diversions.

The London season promises to be a brilliant one. There have not been so few houses to let in the West End for a long time, and the reason given for the defi- ciency is--,so mauy pe.-iple let last year that they can afford to occupy their own houses this. Members of Parliament requiring' extensive accommodation have experienced some difficulty in meeting their wants. For some weeks past, modistes have been very busy designing Court trains for the 'February and March Drawing Rooms, and it is safe to predict that some very lovely gowns will be seen at Buckingham Palace on these occasions. As far as the debutantes are concerned white net or tulle, over satin, will be chiefly employed for their skirts, while their trans will be of white bro- cade or white satin, as of old. Flowers will be lavishly used for trimming, and in many instances the extreme ends of the trains will be ornamented by cascaded draperies of lace and bunches cf 'flowers or feathers, the trimmings being carried, invariably, straight across, in- -stead of being grouped as they were last year, at one corner only. 'China came seems likely to obtain a wide vogue this coming spring and summer. It was in, really, all last season, in a way—that is, evening frocks and some few Me dresses were built of it. The fabric is one that dyes most variedly and :most excellently, and therefore to the economically minded, offers the chance of change. The woman who is in the habit of making narrow shoulder straps do duty for sleeves, would do well to discover if her arm" harmonises," that is to say, if the sipper and lower parts are proportionally developed. If shiiefinds they are not she will he wise to patronise the long transparent sleeves now permissible, and desigaed for these whose arms are not all they might be, in the matter of shape and colour. There are some old fashioned people—who are however credited with excellent taste— who-claim that the classical ideal is quite as good a typo as any that prevails nowadays,and that the arms of the Venuede Medici for instance oueht to satisfy the most ambitious woman of to-day. Here are her messure- ments :—,Length from shoulder to finger tips, 28 7-2 in.; length from sheulder to elbow, 123 in; leagth from elbow to finger tips;lsiin.; round the upper arm.lll ; round the elbow, 10* in.; round the forearm, 91 in.; round the wrist, '5 7-16 in. Althletic women may pre- fer to fellow the model of Diana, who in one statue has arms moulded on the followine proportions :—Length from shoulder to finger tips, 29 7-10 in.:7, from shoulder to elbow, 'l5 1;5 in,; from elbow to Enger tipe,flB* in.; round the upper arm, 16i in.: round the forearm, 9 15-16 ; round the wrist, 5 5-16 in.

There is very little foundation for the reproach hurleel againstthe modern woman, that she no longer cares to be a good needlewoman. We have less time, in these athletic days to devote to the needle, but to say ve have altogether forsaken the art is a stavliment far removed from the truth. That we spend more time in the fresh air, and toss in sitting indoors stitching, is, of course, a well-known fact,but few would care to disoutethe benv- ficial effect this change has trought about, We no longer faint on the smallest possible provocation, good healthy complexions and appetites too, are much mere common than was the case when our mothers and grandmothers were young. And even if we do become less nimble with the needle as time goes on it is surely better to e enjoy good health than the distinction of being a goad; needlewornatt, desirable as the accomplishment snay be., Just now the reproduction of guasian,Bulgarian, and Celtic embroideries is claiming our attention, as well as the charming old "print "work .of centuries ago, 4311- tardy carried out in black and white—the ground white, the etching black silk—special fawn being accorded to the reproduction of etchings of anitnals,the works of the best artists of the day. For the centres of dinner tables the intricate satin stitch is being worked in Bulgarian patterns with shades of yellow leaves, the linen ground scarcely visible. CHESTER.

, FIRE AT THE MAYOR'S OFFICES, SANKEY-mu= IShortly after nine o'clock on Tuesday morning a fire was found to have broken out in the offices of Alderman J. E. Wright in Sankey-street, where he carries on the business of architect and surveyor. Information was immediately conveyed to the Fire Engine Station, and the bell which has recently been fixed at the station was rung. Superintendent Turner and several men at once proceeded with the handreel to the scene of the fire, and they were fol- lowed shortly afterwards by the steam fire engine, Captain,in charge of Engineer Ratcliffe. Sergeant Chantl.r was on the spot as soon as the outbreak was discovered, and hts and a few others assisted to extinguish the fire with buckets of water. On the arrival of the firemen the hose was attached to the hydrant, and water was at once thrown on the flames, the fire having broken out in the general office, adjoining Mr. J. E. Wright's private office. • The-flames were soon extinguished,and the services of the steam fire engine were not brought into re- luisition. In addition to Alderman Wright's offices, the upper portion of the premises are also occupied by Mr. James Bolton, accoun- tant; Mr. J. R. Williams, accountant; and Mr. F. Cooke. :qr. James Parkinson photographer, oc- cupies the premises below,and valuable goods were damaged by the water which was poured into the offices above. The damage would have been greater had not a large quantity of goods been expedi- tiously removed on the discovery of the outbreak. The studio escaped any damage whatever, and will therefore not interfere with his business. The fire is supposed to be due ton defective flue, and to have been smouldering for some hours. A number of books and some shelves were damaged, although the firemen succeeded in saving a number of letter books, memoranda, &c. The police were early on the scene, and rendered valuable assistance.

THE E A MIN EP, FRIDAY, FEBRUARY 18, 1898.

THE AMERICAN COTTON TRADE.

(BY Ma. H. WHITHAM).

Let us now turn to the cotton strike that is now on in the New England States. When the English Cotton operatives hear that a strike is on among their brethren of the now England States of America and affecting from 200,000 to 300,000 workers they will be somewhat surprised, and may wonder how it all comes about. With the press, and the politicians who are on top, unceasingly boasting of the good times that have come with the McKinley regime it certainly does seem strange how it comes about that strikes and reductions are as mach the rule as they were three years ago. The history of this strike dates from away back. Two years ago I took up a southern paper, and therein found an article with the caption in big type" Big profits in cotton manufacturing." After reading it I remember lay- ing it down with the thought "Yes,and another big source of trouble." At that time in one county in South Carolina there were seventeen large cotton factories,and quite a number of others being built. These factories cost as much as 500,000d015. and some of them have a pay roll of 30,000d015.a month. The Dingley tariff has helped forward the Southern cotton manufacture to a great degree. New mills have bon going up steadily. More than 14,000,000 pounds have been invested in the cotton business. From Lynchburg, Vancouver, to Atlanta, Georgia, there is an almost. continuous line of cotton fac- tories, and they dot the cotton belt as far south as New Orleans.

These southern mills are surrounded by the cot- ton fields, and the cotton is brought almost directly from the field to factory. The mills here are ship- ping largely to China. This southern section of the country has an export of nearly £20,000 each month and it is largely made up of cotton. The Southern manufacturers are very rewired about their busi- ness, but I have seen it stated that there are few businesses that are paying so well as that of the cotton mills. Take the Pacolet mills, which are located within twelve miles of here. They have now a plant which experts say is worth more than 1,000,000d018. They have a surplus of about 500,000, dole, and they have for years been paying semi- annual dividends of 5 per cent. These mills were organised in 1883, with a capital of 300,000501 e, which has been increased to 700,000d015. The mills made 26,000d01e, profit the first year they were in existence, and since then they have paid out more than 380,000d018 in dividends. They started with one mill, but this has since been increased to three, and the Aurplas is nearly twice the amount of its original capital stock. These mills eat up about eighty bales of cotton every day, and they make every year M3CI than 30.000,000 yards of cotton cloth. The same conditions exist in Georgia. All kinds of coloted goods,shirtings,towelllog and ticking are turned out, giving employment to thousands of people. The city of Augusta has over 10,000,000 dollars invested in factories,and it id said a part of its output goes to England. No complaints are heard in the south by the em- ployers. They have it all their oWn way. There are no unions to contend with, and to anyone who knows anything of the poor Southern whites this is not to be wondered at. There are no strikes,and no laws restricting the working hours. Ihe hands work from eleven to twelve hours, and they are al- most all white people,and the supply of labor is al- most unlimited. Two years ago the South brit! in round numbers about 500 cotton mills, and the amount of money invested was about £24.000,000. If we go still further south we find that in !axles the cotton business is flourishing, and that from 20 to 60 per cent profits are made, and that more mils are going up. Mexico's importations of Cotton cloth from England during the past ten months have declined 7,862,600 yards or 19 per cent contrasted with imports for thY same time last year. Imports to Mexico from the United States have decreased 25 per cent. •What lam trying to keep weilin sight is the sure fact that trade is find- ing its home in the lowest wage paying centres,and in those countries where civilization is at its lowest and where Parliaments have not yet begun to care for the laborer. In Mass, a demand is to be made on the Legislature to repeal the factory re- striction laws relating/ to the hours of labor. If this is successful it will be a back down. It will bring the factory hand down under the heel of rt more merciless and cut throat industrial contest tbau has ever before been experienced. Wages are4o per cent lower in the Southern mills than in the New England mills.A committee of New England manu- facturers say in a report that its chief difficulty has been in finding terms sufficiently strong " to set forth the hopelessness of a prolonged compe- tition between northern and southern manufac- turers under the conditions that now prevail." The report goes on to say. "The advantages, how- ever, which pertain to labor are very great; the long hours run and the low prices paid, we believe,make the cost of labor in the South about 40 per cent lees than in the North. The working day in North Carolina is 12 hours, 24 per cent longer than in Massachusetts, and the price paid per day for com- mon labor in the mills is from 50 to 75 cents." Be- -coming more specific respecting the difference in wages, between North and South the report says:— "We obtained prices for labor in several mills, and feel that we are making a conservative statement in saying that there is a difference between the prices paid there and in Massachusetts of at least 40 per cent. For instance the prices paid speeder tenders varied from 3905018. to 4 805o1s. per week of 66 hours. Our prices are from 7dole, to'9.sodols. for 58 I hours. In submitting this statement we think a better basis for comparison can be given by taking a given number of spindles, and showing the cost of running them in both places. The cost of running 112 slabber spindles, 408 intermediate and 408 fine, in one mill we visited, was 26•70d01e. tor 66 hours. One hand runs two slubbera of 56 spindles each at 75 cents per day, three hands run four intermediate frames of 192 spindles each, at SO cents per day each, and two hands run three fine frames of 136 spindles each at 65 Cents per day each. In one of the newest and best arranged northern mills the same number of spindles of each kind is run the same length of time for 3514d015. a price 34 per cent more than in the southern mill. In another northern mill, not so well arranged and older, the same work costs 43,29d015, which is 62 per cent more than in the southern mill." I think I have now shown pretty clearly how it comes about that thousands of New England's cotton operatives are now out on strike. They,like the English trade unionists, are asked to give up all their organizations have fought and won for them. They are asked to give up all that science and dis- covery has done during the past lifty years. We know that we shall have to earn our bread by the sweat of our brow, but is it nothing that man has achieved a mastery over natural forces never before known, and that lie has attained the power to satisfy the wants of his physical being with a less and less expenditure of labour. Where does the benefit from science and invention come in if there is to be no immunity from the bar I and unremit- ting labour which former con litions made neces- sary The face of labour is to be ground, and a waste- ful contest of capital is to go on producing what we have already in superabundance. No heed is given to the question of a sensible system of distribution so that the shoeless, the coatless, and the hungry may be sent away satisfied. The question now is, Ihow soon may we hope to get down to the lowest depth, so that we may have a fresh start?

Lord Rosebury's ball on Monday next to celebrate the "coming oat" of his daughters,the Ladies Sybil and Margaret Primrose is to be a magnificent aff,iir. The company ie to be a young one, although a point has been stretched in favour of the Prince of Wales.

Before the Royal Commission the evidence was simply overwhelming in favour of a better system of revising mineral royalties: Mr. Fenwick, M.P. mentioned the Choppington Colliery, in I Northumberland which wag stepped for months. The masters wanted to reduce the men's wages by 2i per I too. The men offered to accept a reduction of 1 per ' ton. As this was not enough, hundreds of men were thrown out of employment. There is no record that the landlord reduced the royalty. Mr. John Wilson, M.P., mentioned similar cases at Wheatley Hill, Blaydon, and Sacristan. Mr. W. H. Patterson, then secretary of the Durham miners, also gave three cases of mines which had to stop through inability ta pay the royalties. HP said that, between 1875 and 1878, 58 collieries were stopped and 85 partially stopped in the County of Durham. He would not go so far as to say that the high rents were the sole reason for these stoppages, bet it was the opinion of the men that had the royalty rents been reduced during the depression in the coal trade many of the collieries would have been saved. Tha Secretary to the Cumberland Coal Miners gave a case from his own experience. A colliery was closed whose minimum rent was £7OO a year. There was overpaid for coal never less than £5,500. The men were willing to takers reduction of ald a ton, and if the royalty-owner would have done the same the work could have gene on. But this he refused to do, and there was no power to compel him, as obviously there ought to be. One last illustration of this serious injustice. Mr. I. D. Kendal, previously referred to, instanced a case where a landlord fixed the royalty at 7& per ton when iron ore was selling at 3010 365. When prices fell to 141 he did not reduce his royalty, and the leases had to be aban- doned. Assuredly, then, we need Land Courts in England, too, to deal with an abuse of the rights of pro- perty which has such various consequences to both capi- tal and labour.

BUT WROTE FOR INFORMATION AND 31. ESCAPED THE NECESSITY.

Mrs. Hannah Powell had been ill for two years and her symptoms were alarming, the stomach swelling enormously and causing frightful pain. She was an out- patient at the Metropolitan Free Hospital. " I got such a dreadful size," she said. " that the doctors there ad- vised me to go into St. Bartholomew's and undergo an operation." She was prepared for the operation, but it was deferred for ten days, and, unnerved she told the surgeons that she could not undergo the operation. Her [ illness was attributed to ulceration of the stomach. The surgeon, who spoke very kindly and sympathe- tically, appeared to agree that her prospects of life were not lilufficieat ta make it worth while, and he advised her to defer the operation

until it should become inevitable. She appears to have derived benefit, bit early in last year the swelling returned, and on Good Friday she was compelled to take to her bed,where she rem tined tor throe weeks, suffering excruciating pain. See was unable to turn in bed, and the doctors said that they could not do any more for her. Mrs. Powell was thinking of submitting to opera- tion, but having heard of some remarkable cures effected by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, she wrote to Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, 46, Holborn-viaduct, to Inquire whether these Pills would be likely to do her any good. NATIONALISATION OF MINERALS.

By JOSEPH RYDER, General Secretary of the Land Nationalisation Societ y.

ARTICLE XVI. WAYLEAVES.

" Wayleaves "area very remarkable part of the charges which the monopolist of the land impose upon the mining industrv. In the aggregate they ammnt to no less than £220,000 a year. If such a thing be pos- sible, they constitute an extortion that is even worse than that of mineral rents and royalties. Wayleaves are of two kinds—under ground and surface. They are the price per ton paid to different landlords for the privilege of carrying the minerals un ler or over his land. Let us suppose the case of a coal mine where perhaps the distant workings are a mile away from the bottom of the shaft. The coal taken thence may have to pay half a dozen wayleaves to half a dozen dif- ferent landlords, besides the original royalty, on its way to the shaft. Thus often a landlord may have had all the coal taken out of his property, but he may still draw a very respectable income in wayleaves, simply because the main tunnel runs through it. And then, when the coal (or ore) reaches the surface, it often happens that it has to be carried a considerable, distance to the rail- way or the wharf. So here, again, is another chance for the übiquitous landlord. And it will be easily realised that in some easel the rent, royalty, an wayleaves to- gether will equal the wages which have been paid to the men who with strenuous labour have hewed the mineral Some important and valuable evidence was given on these points by Mr. J. D. Kendal, a mining engineer and mine lessee. He knew of many cases where prohibitory wayleaves were demanded. One particularly in which one proprietor &Orel such a rate that they were obliged togs another way, and spend about twice the money In making double the length of line. Vet after that, al- though they had three or four different properties to go over, meaning so many different royalties to pay, they got a muck lessi wayleave n the aggregate than the landlord asked for himself alone. He mentioned another case in which he was directly concerned. The landlord charged the full fee-simple for the land, and,in charged a penny per ton wayleave. As 120,000 tons were tarred over the land every year, the landlord's toll was £5OO a year beyond the ordinary market valve of the land. Still another inetance of EIRGALISED BIACRNIAIL.

A raised ganeyway was made at the expense of the lessee,of course. The agricultural value of the land was scarcely lessened at all. But the lessee had to pay a way i.ave of 4.1 per ton. The width of the strip of land was only 20 yards. When asked by the RoyalCommissicners if there were any exceptional circumstances to account : for that extortionate charge, " No," said par. Kendal "except that the man who pays it had no 0- ance of, getting out without paying it he was in a cleft stick." Now,it is a common defence of this kind of thing that if there be any hardship the lessee has no right to com- plain, because be made the bargain with his eyes open, and must abide by the circumstances. But those who reason thus forget one very important fact—that be- tween landlord and tenant there is no freedom of con- tract. The former holds in his legal grip a neces- sity of life which is fi"l in quantity. He has an absolute monopoly. The latter, to uSe the expressive language above mentioned. "is in a cleft stick." He has a choice of " Take it or leave It" ; a choice of two evils. When a traveller is waylaid by an armed highwayman, and he deliberately prefers to hand over his purse in order to escape with his life, every one will see that he does so beeauge he is not a free arrent. similar in all essentials is the above case of landlord and tenant. It is not a bargain between equals. And no greater proof of the landlords' superior strength to secure their own interests need be given than the laud legislation of Ireland and the 930ttiah High- lands, which was the result of agitation among the rack- rented tenants, to whom freedom of contract was un- known. The landlords can always screw the rent up high enough without State aid. But the tenants had to invoke the aid of the Watts to protect them from ruin. And, pending land nationslisation, this principle will bare to ha extended to the tenants of other part i of the United Kingdom, and he applied not only to agricultural and town lands, but also to _mineral lands.

FOREIGN COMPETITION.

Tnere remains an important question, that of foreign competition. England's pre-eminent position in the iron and steel industry is being challenged by her Continental and American rivals, but more especially by the former. la Germany and Blgium our competitors have a great advantage in their system of State railways, which charge much lighter rates than do our own private rail- ways. I have seen it stated that this difference is s-ime- times nearly 10i a ton in favour of out rivals. This,then partly accounts for the way in which our iron raile,ateel plates, girders, /moire being undersold even in our own markets. Then there is the lower rate of wage that pre- vails on the Continent. But beyond both these, we are handicapped by the difference -between the mineral royalties. Let us take the case cf the manufactured article, whether if he a rail, a ship-plate, or a girder, and reckon the total amount of the royalties on the coal, limo and ore out of which it is made. In Great Britain a ton of pig iron will pay in royalties from 3s. in Cleveland to 61. 31. in Scotland. Cumberland, or Lancashire. In Germany the royalty is about 61. ; in France 51. ; an .1 in Belgium about the same. In Great Britain a ton of iron ship-plates will pay a fax to the landlord of ss. 91. But the corresponding royalty in Germany, France, and Belgium would be is. only to the State. In Great Bri- tain a tun of steel rails would pay a tax of Irons 34. 81. to Ts. 31. to the landlord ;wilo in the other countries the State would only charge from 81. to lid. Thus it will be seen how British industry is handicapped by the land monopoly. And if we can do so well in spite of this hindrance to success, it is reasonable to assume that a great impetus would he gi Yen to our trade if we com- peted on equal terms. On the Continent the sovereignty of the State over the minerals has long been rec#gniaed ; and although the Continental system is far from perfect, yet it is much better than our own.

===== 0002601_18980218_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
A REDUCTION OF TAXATION OF £309,000.

It must not be overbokod that, under State owner- Ship of the minerals, not only are the rovalties more reasonable, but, whether small or great, they are paid into the national exchequer, and so benefit the whole body of taxpayers, In this country they are exceaSiVe, and simply enrich private individuals. If the Kentish coalfield could be profitably worked the national puree would in no wise benefit so long a the pres.mt system lasts. The only result would be that a ,umber of Kentish landowners would revel in an aoefAsion ut un- earned increment, which would doubtless he as welcome as was their share of the relief which Lord Salisbury gave them last year out of the pockets of the urban taxpayers. In conclusion, then, it must be clear that ,we shall have to imitate our chief eompetitora by nationalising our mineral resources. First,let us have a Parliamentary declaration that all minerals at pres nt undiscovered belong to the Crown. This would bs a valuable measure, costing nothing and hurting nobody. Then let Land Courts be established to regulate the royalties and mine rents according to the state of trade, counting the wages of labour and the remuneration of eapital as the first charges upon the trade. Probably a fair settlement of the amount nf the royalties to be paid ' would reduce it to £3,000,000 a year. Thrn let this amount contribute its fair share to th" tsxati,n of the country. And let it be understood that the landlord tribute i not to go on for ever, but is to he eirb,r ex- changed for redeemable land bonds or for terminable annuities. In this way we may, all to tardily, but better late than never, repair the damage that was done when the State allowed the minerals to lapse icto private hands.

LATEST PRESS OPINION.

---- " Western Telegraph," of January 1, 1898 'Few houses in Manchester occupied in the same line of business are better known or enjoy a larger share of patronage than that of Samuel Pitts, late of Gaythorn, now of [London-road, Manchester, the completo house shop, and office furnisher. It was in 1845 that the present proprietor initiated the business, operations having been first com- menced by him in Brook-street as a cabinet maker and dealer in office furniture. By his energy and ability the concern prospered, and more accommodation becoming necessary, a move was made to the late premises in Gaythern about 26 years since. Samuel Pitts soon acquired a name for the superior excellence of the goods he supplied, the vast range of selection offered to customers, and the house advanced with rapid strides into a foremost and leading position in the trade. Juat recently the Corporation purchased the Gay- thorn premises for street improvement purposes. and Samuel Pitts had once again to find fresh fields and pastures new.' This time his choice tell upon the vast edifice until latterly known as Borough Buildings, situate 100 to 120. London-road, which he has now converted into what is often termed the largest and most convenient general furnishing establishment in Man- chester. Ihe premises occupied are enormous in exteLt and attractive in appearance and are prominently and com- mandingly located. They comprise a magnificent Montag() of plate-glass windows, well stocked with high-class goods, together with extens.ve showrooms nearly 300 feet long on the ground floor, and offices and counting house. The first, second, and third floors are occupied by a number of stock and showrooms, and extended workshop accommodation is offered by the premises on the opposite side of London-road. The establishment throughout has been admirably arranged, and thoroughly and elegantly fitted up with every require- ment, including elect! ie light throughout, hydraulic paa- senger and luggage lift to all floors, for the effective display of the splendid selection of goods, which cannot be surpassed by those of any competitor, either in extent, variety, or general superiority. In soundness of material, good work- manship, and elegance and novelty of &sign the house has no successful rivals in Manchester. while prices will always be found of the most satisfactory character. The proprietor is thoroughly acquainted with every eligible source of supply and in addition to his own special make the establishment displays the choicest specimens, both new and second-hand, of other well-known manufacturers. Samuel Pitts is a thoroughly practical man, and his personal supervision is bestowed upon the business in its entirety, He is able, ener- getic, and courteous in his control, and all his dealings are marked by his methods of strict fairness and honesty. In conclusion. we should strongly advise all furnishing, whether In part or throughout, to pay this establishment a visit. The same civility and courtesy is extended to all, whether buying or not, and the firm's well-known straightforward, syste- matic meth;xl ensures all orders being satisfactorily exe- cuted. Tith EXA.IVIINEIi.

FEBRUARY 18t, 1898.

IT can hardly be said that the pro- Parliament. ceedings in Parliament this week have been of a lively or even of a deeply interesting character. The engineers' lock- out is now a thing of the past, but anything that transpires with regard to it in the House of Com- mons is bound to be followed with the closest attention by large numbers of the working people lof the country. During the progress of the lock- lout some serious charges were made against the I Admiralty, to the effect that it was favouring the employers by allowing some of them to delay the completion of tkeir contracts for war vessels and for other warlike material. On Tuesday, Mr. GOSCHEN was questioned upon this point by Mr. E. ROBERTSON, who held an office at the Admiralty under the last Liberal Govern- ment. Mr. ROBERTSON asked whether the Admiralty had decided that the contrac- tors whose work had been delayed by a lock-out were entitled to the benefit of the strike clause, nd it must at once be said that Mr. GOSCHEN.S reply was not much to the point though it contains some of the ingenuity of the special pleader. If the view he takes holds good it -is quite evident that manufacturers of war material need pay very little attention to any strike clause that may be inserted in their cm- tracts with at least one public department. "If,' says Mr. GOSCHEN, "a labour war commences with a strike on one side which is followed by a partial lock-out on the other, is it to the lock-out or to the strike that the delay in work is due. If a firm locks out 25 per cent, of its men with the intent ion of carrying on its work with the rest, and the rest—the other 75 per cent.—then go out on strike, is the resultant stoppage of work due to the lock-out or the strike? Again, if there is no strike or lock-out in some other quarter, what is the position of the shipbuilding firm ?" The whole tenour of this evasive reply will,we have no doubt,convince at least nine out of ten of the more intelligent artisans of the country that the bias at the Admiralty was on the side of the employers and against the workmen. When a firm locks-out 25 per cent of its engineers and the other 75 leave the workshop at the same time as their discharged comrades, the authority is in doubt whether this is a lock-out or a strike. Yet where does the doubt come in? The firm that locks-out a quarter of its workmen knows beforehand that this means the voluntary cessation of labour by the other threequarters and it acts upon that know- ledge. That such an instance as this is, in its essential character, a lock-out and not a strike is shewn by the fact that if the 75 per cent did not voluntarily leave work according to custom and rule, they themselves would be discharged within at least the three following weeks. Mr. ROBERT- sox has given notice that he will again raise this subject of the strike clause in the House of Commons and it certainly deserves the notice of trades unionists, and, indeed, workmen generally. What may be described as the political question of the week was the debate on the Government's Indian frontier policy. This debate was opened with an amendment to the address moved from the Liberal benches by Mr. LAWSON Weraorr. The line of attack adopted by the Liberals is indicated by the terms of the amendment which , ;expressed disapproval of what is known as the ' forward policy," and an opinien that the safety and prosperity of the Indian Empire will be best promoted by respecting the independence of the frontier tribes, and avoiding the occupation of their territory. Mr. LAWSON WALTON'S speech is variously described by various London correspon- dents. Mr. WALTON has won a high reputation as a lawyer, and according to the Parliamentary reporter of the "Manchester Guardian" his eloquence is of the modern Parliamentary style. It is "unemotional, unrhetorical, a mass of Closely-reasoned, well-ordered arguments, depend- ing for its force on logic and lucidity." In this instance the orator had a splendid case and he seems to have made almost the best of it.

At all events, Lord GEORGE HAMILTON, the Secre- tary for India, who had to follow on behalf of the Government evidently felt extremely ill at ease, and sought relief by some rather extravagant abuse of the other side. He promised to dispose of the Chitral case in five minutes but as a matter of fact he had not finished with it in half-an-hour, The : outbreak along the frontier was not according to this Minister, caused by any breach of faith on the part of the British or Indian Go vernment but simply, as he vaguely and pictur- esquely put it, by "frenzy in the air and fanatic- ism running like wild-fire in the mountain." The most notable part of his speech came, however, towards the finish. It was virtually a confession that a great blunder had been made and an appeal to th 3 Opposition to help the Government to alter its course and carry out a wiser policy. The Goverment has apparently been forced to the conclusion that the military party in India has had too much of its own way, and it now wants the Opposition to help to regain that complete control which it should never have relinquished. This debate will certainly help to improve the Liberal position, both in Parliament and in the country. The Pembrokeshire election again emphasises the faciV that the Tories are rapidly loing their hold of the constituencies, and there can be no doubt that the Indian frontier blunder forms one of the most effective of the arguments used at these very suggestive bye-elections.

THE blowing up of the United A Warship Destroyed. States battle-ship, Maine, in Havana Harbour has caused a sen- sation throughout the civilised world. The most important question in connection with this de- plorable disaster is, whether the Maine has been destroyed by accident or design. As everyone knows the political relations between the United States and Spain have been very much strained of late by the continuance of the Cuban rchellion,and as a matter of fact the Maine was despatched to Havana for the purpose of watching the course of events, though ostensibly its visit to the Cuban, harbour was of a purely friendly character. If it turns out that the American ironclad has been wilfully torpedoed by Spaniards or Cubans, it will probably be utterly impossible for President Mac" NL EY and his government to resist the storm of indignation that will be roused in the States. Therefore, it is seareely too much to say that a question of peace or war depends upon the unra- velling of this mystery. The accident itself has been as calamitous as it was sudden. It is believed that between two and three hundred sailors have been killed outright by the explosion while a large number of others have been dreadfully injured.

The local fund in aid of the distress in the West of Ireland is now closed. It has resulted in the sending of £278 in money and £5O or £6O worth of • clothing to the afflicted districts, and, on the whole therefore,it cannot be said that Warrington has not done its share towards the relief of the distress. We notice that at the tea meeting at St. John's Presbyterian Church on Thursday last week, Mr. R A. Naylor, who presided, seemed to be disposed to generalise upon the fact that the distress is in a Catholic and not in a Protestant part of Ireland. This way of looking at the question ap-- pears to have a great attraction for some people though why it should be almost exclusively applied .1 to Ireland is rather puzzling. Thera is distress in the West Indies as well as in the West of Ireland, and that distress is to be relieved by a large im- perial dole, yet there are no invidious reflections upon the prevailing creel of the West Indian is- landers. It is not long since a very substantial fund was raised in aid of the people of Essex, and the distress in that county , was in no way connected with religious beliefs. A study of the political and economic history of Ireland would be useful reading for those who are inclined to jump to hasty conclu- sions, for they might then begin to realise that the results of a long period of political misgovernment and economic oppression are not got rid of in one• generation nor in two. The records of every British colony prove that Irishmen, Catholic as well as Pro— testant, rise to the highest levels of citizenship, when they are placed in an environment which allows of the full de'velopment of all their qualities,

There is abundant evidence in a report which we. give in another column, that the Warrington and District Nursing Association is already doing a. great amount of useful work in 117arringtona is a work which is carried on very unpbtrusively and probably unnoticed by a large section of the public but the value of it can not be over-estimated. That it is thoroughly appreciated by those who get the benefit of it is shown by the superintendent's re- port to the committee. The language in which some of the patients express their gratitude to the nurses has the ring of sincerity about it. "One pa- tient,' says the superintendent, "expressed the con- viction that there would be a golden crown' for her nurse, it only for what she had done for berguad another,a poor helpless hopeless chronic,pathetically promised, " I'll do as much for you nume, when. I get better."

The impolicy of allowing private companies to undertake public supplies—whether it be the supply of water, gas or lectricity—will bs brought home to the people of Sheffield by the price which the Corporation of the city has now to pay in order to buy out the local electric lighting company. This company was formed only live years ego with a capital of £60,000, but has prospered so much that the Corporation has now to pay at the rate of .£213 Ss. for every £lOO share originally . invested, and in addition £70,000 for stock and stores in hand and recent capital expenditure. Warrington has had some experience in buying out gas and water companies, but fortunately the Corporation intend to have the electric supply in its own hands from the commencement. By the way, we notice from "The Surveyor'and Municipal and County Engineer" of last week that the neighbouring borough of St. Helens is about to undertake a scheme of refuse destruction, combined with the construction of an electrical generating station ,f,,r the purpose of providing electrical traction for the whole of its tramways. St.nelens will also during the year lay out two new recreation grounds,one of 20 acres,and the other of 12 acres and extend its abattoir luta add to the refrigerating plant in that very useful inetitutien.

RUNCORN PICE'WICIC LITERARY SOCIETY. At the weekly meetiog of the above society on Tues- day evening, Mr. T. Saunders in the chair, a paper was read on the "Riddle of life" by vl r. A. Dodd. A discus- sion took place, all the members taking part.

FIRE AT RUNCORN. About 7.30 on Sunday night as Miss Woodland was lighting a gas in one of the bedrooms at her father's house, High-street, Runcorn,the curtains accidently got on fire and burnt the venetian blind, which was hang- ing over the window. A few other small things were burnt. The fire brigade turned out under the deputy superintendent Mr. R. Wright, but their services were not required as the fire had been distinguished by Sergeant Gittins of the Volunteers and P.C. Gregory. The damage is estimated at about £2 which is covered by insurance in the Irish National Fire Insurance Com- pany. •

ACCIDENT TO GEORGE WAINWRIGHT, RUNCORN. On Tuesday afternoon, George Wainwright, who was a member of the Runcorn Football Club for many years, and who also represented his county on many occasions met with an accident whilst following his employment at the Old Quay works. He was superintending the re- move( of the roof of the lime shed when he was struck on ths forehead by a falling spar, the force of the blow knocking him to the ground. It Was found that he had received a serious wound in the forehead, and he re- ceived surgical attention.

LAUNCH ON A NSW STEAMER NOR A RUNCORN Shirowwita. On Thursday there was launched horn the shipbuild- ing yard of 'Messrs. S. McKnight and Co., Limited, of Ayr, a steel screw steamer built to the order of Mr. William Rowland, bill powner ko., of Liverpool and Rim- corn. As the steamer left the ways she was christened "Florence" by Miss Florence Rowland, daughter of the owner, the pleasing ceremony being gracefully per- formed. The steamer has been designed by Mr. Edward Rennaugh, of Liverpool, and is intended for the general coasting trade. The engine and boiler have been built by Messrs. Ross attd Duncan, of Olesgew,and the vessel is expected to attain a speed of eleven or 12 knots an hour. The dimensions of the vessel are—length 141 ft. Sin.; breadth, 24ft.; depth mAulded, lift. Thee carrying capacity will be abut 400 tons.

POOR LAW OFFICERS-SUPERANNUATION ACT. The Local Government Board forwarded a circular letter stating that having regard to the opinion given by the Law Officers of .the Crown they considered that the "salary and emoluments"within the meaning of the Act of the clerk to a board of guardians do not include fees received by him as returning officer either (1) at elections of guardians whether before or after the Local Government Act, 1894, or (2) at the first election of Parish Councillors, or (3) at the first election of Rural District Councillors, or (4) at ,the election of School Boards ; that the returning officer at the election of Guardians is not,fur the purposes of the Act,an officer of tho guardians ; and that the" salary and emoluments" of a clerk to the guardians,who, under section 81 of the Local Government Act 1894,h01ds the office of clerk to the Rural District Council, do not include his salary or emoluments as clerk to the Rural District Council ; that only the emoluments of officers or employments actually held by the officer or servant at the time of his retire- ment can be taken into account in calculating, for the purposes of section 3 of the Act, the average amount of the salary or wages and emoluments of the officer or servant during the five years referred to in the section.

Mr. 'Walter Bewen, clerk to the Birmingham Guar- dians,wrote ennlosing resolutions and in siting the Run- corn Union to adopt similar ones:dealing with the above question. They were convinced of the urgent need for legislation on this question.—Mr. Williams said he was strongly in favour of some legislation in that direction. They all knew from experience on that Board that a very large amount of poverty had its origin from drun- kenness. They also knew that there were habitual i drunkards in their midst who were a nuisance to society and a curse to Their own families, and who ultimately became chargeable to this union. He proposed that they pass a similar resolution to the ones Birmingham had passed. The Board knew well that if any action was taken by the Guardians the liberty of the subject would not be infringed.—Mr. Aaron seconded and the proposition was carried.

THE CONSTRUCTION OF A PIPE SEWER IN HATTON. The following letter was read from Mr. J. Lyon Whittle, Town Clerk of Warrington :—lt has been re- pOrted to the Water Committee of this Corporation that Mr. Swinton, your surveyor, is constructing a pipe sewer in the township of Hatton,and that it is intended to place the outfall into Hatton brook above the intake of the Warrington Waterworks in the township of Appleton. The Corporation have the power aud do im- pound the water from Hatton brook,which is within the limits of their supply. My committee feel very strongly in the matter, and have given me instructions to pro- ceed by injunction against all persons offending, and if the sewer is being constructed by the authority or direc- tion of the Runcorn Dintrict Council I shall have to take proceedings against them.l must ask you to let me have a reply to this letter as soon as possible, as the matter permits of no delay.”—lt was decided to appoint a corn- mittee to poet the Town Clerk to amicably settle the matter.
