RUNCORN CO-OPEPATrVE SOCIErY.

ANNUAL TEA PARTY.

The annual tea party in connection with the above society was held on Wednesday evening in the Co-opera- tive Reoms,Church-street, Runcorn. There Was a very large attendance, and the tables which were well filled with eatables were presided over by the following ladies: —Mesdames R. Stubbs,Moores, Ledstone Worrall, Raw- linson,Gibbons, Nickson, Henshall, Pendlebury, Bewyer, Rushton, Davies, and Elliot, end the Misses Moores, Stubbs (2), Brockley, Rhodes, and Withington. About eight o'clock, after tea was concluded, a grand concert was given in the Public Hall. The room was packed from end to end and a pleasant evening was spent. There were on the platform Meesrs.R. H. Poenett(chairo man), Dudley, Riley, Stubbs,Moore, Dean, Willful, Dyer, Hartles, Rawlinson, Ledstone, Shaw (secretary), and Morris (manager. The first item on the programme was the Chairman's address. rb said he had been in Runcorn ten years and he had often read the reports of the meetings of till Co-operative Society in the local newspapers. He knew they had men of Mel- nese at their head, men with brains, men with zeal and energy, and it was their duty be back these men up. It was not their duty to criticise and to be cheese-paring in their policy, for such a policy had never won any- thing in England or anywhere else. Co-operation was advancing as fast as ever. Something new was being discovered every day. It was only 30 years ago that chloroform was discovered. That was perhaps the great- est discovery of this century. The thing that relieved pain was discovered by Mr. Simpson. To-day in their colleges the students had given over reading Mr. Simp- son's book, because they had got newer books. The sub- ject had grown, and fresh light had been thrown upon it. The same thing applied to every department of life, and those rules which governed the co-operative move- ment in the days of their forefathers were no longer suited be the times in which they now lived. Re believed they had men connected with the Co-operative move- ment—he knew two or three, viz., Mr. Johnsen, of Glasgow, and Mr. Butcher, of Leicester—who had fine intellects and excellent brain powers. When they had such men it was their duty to back them up with all the force and enthusiasm they could command. No man however strong or powerful of himself could run a movement of this kind unless he had the confidence and support of all those connected with it. If they lived up to the ideal of their lives they would never say fail no matter what their position might be. There was as much chance for their children to-day,as there was in the days of their forefathers. (Applause.) The second item on the programme Was a duett "Ex- celsior" by Messrs. Dockrey and Shiogler. Mr. Thurlow H. Johnson, humourist, gave " Laughing," character sketch "Sarah Jane's Top Note," humourous song, "The one legged family." For each item he was loudly en- cored. He kept the audience in uncontrollable laughter all the time he was before them. The other items were. song "Angus MacDonald," Mrs. Tirnperley ; song "Ho! Jolly Jenkin," Mr. T. Johnson; song" The Better Land," Mr. J. W. Dockroy; "Queen of the earth,"Mr. A. Sning- ler; "The Lost Chord," Mr. J. W. Dockrey; and "The Old Soldier," Mr. Shiegler. Mr. William Cooke A.R.C.O filled the duties uf accompserst.All the artistes acquitted themselves very creditably and were all applauded. During the Interval Mr. Dudley,chairman of the Local Committee, made a brief speech. He said the chairman had alluded to the fact that although Chloroform had only been discovered 30 years, it was being superceded by other achievements of science. It was 80 years since co-operation was introduced, and,itwas not getting played out. The reason was that co-operation wag the people's own movement. The Co-operation movement had caused prices to be kept at one steady rate which I had enabled many a private trader.to make hislortune,l Co-operetors were getting more respectable. The origina- tors were not in the same happy position that co-opera- tors were to.day, but they owed their position to those who introduced the movement. They were now better housed, better fed and better educated, and all because they had shewn that they could manage their own busi- ness. In Runcorn they had a business which represented £lO,OOO a year. They all welcomed Mr. Posnett amongst them that evening and they would all admit that to get such a gentleman to preside was a step in the right direction. (Applause). Mr. Posnett was the worthy son offa worthy sire, the worthy head of a worthy firm, and it was that clues of firms they wanted in Runcorn and the country generally. On their behalf he presented Mr. Posnett with a splendidly bound volume of the Co- operative Annual as a tangible proof of their thanks. (Applause.). The Chairman, in acknowledging the gift, said private workers and co-operators were not by any means anta- gonistic. They worked on different lines,but they worked for the same end, for the same advancement, for the same empire, and for the same Queen. (Applause.) He thanked them very much for their kind reception, and for their memente of that interesting occasion, they bad his heartfelt gratitude. (Applause).

The secret of the rrmarkable success of Michael, the little Welsh cyclist, is now explained by the rider him- self. Michael always rides with pacers,whorn he follows at a distance of about six inches,and this, coupled with his small size, enables him to remain continually in the suction of the atmospheric vortmand gives him the ad- vantage of the returning current of air, which, in his case, when following the sextuple machine, gives him, be declares, an advantage of ten seconds to the mile._

Grandmotherly legislation seems to have reacked its furthest limit in Virginia, where an " Anti-flirting Bill " has lately been debated and rejected by the Senate. The object of the measure was to prevent Virginian youths from distracting school-girls' sninds from their studies,' but a modified form forbidding loitering by young men near schools for girls has been passed by the House. The Ohio Legislature ales has views as to the control of the relations of the sexes, a Bill having been introduced re- quiring persons applying for licenses to marry to pass a medical examination. It provides for an examining board of physicians in each county, and forbids the ' granting of a license to persons having dipsomania, a any form of insanity or hereditary tuberculosis.

PROPOSED RECREA.TION GROUND FOR RUNCORN'.

OFFICIAL INQUIRY.

Runcorn Urban District Council having made an application to the Board of Agriculture for a provisional order vesting in them the regulation of Runcorn Heath and Runcorn Hill. Mr. Arthur Russell, an officer of that Board, held an inquiry in Runcorn Public Hall, on Tues. day morning. Mr. F. A. Lake represented the District Council, and Mr. Raymond Barker (Messrs. Walters and Co.) and Mr. C. E. Linaker, the agent, represeqted the Marquis of Cholmondeley. There were also present Messrs. S. Taylor, J.P., C.C., chairman of the Cones:M. Pritchard, G. H. Kirkham,W. Handley, J. R. Frith.Fl J. Giese, J. W. Lightburn, J. Wilding (surveyor), E. Mar- shall (secretary to the Council), W. Sutton (assistant oversesr) and severaJ ratepayer,. Mr. E Marshall proved the posting of the necessary notices of the inquiry. Mr. Russell said the .13 )ard .of Agriculture before they issued an order wished to ascertain the local feeling on the subjecr and hence the Act directed that two meetings should be held on the spot for the purpose of giving the inhabitants an opportunity of stating their views. From the small meeting it did not look as if there was any great excitement on the qaestion at any rate. The applicants for the order were the Runcorn Urban District Council, the Marquis of Cholmondeley, Maitland Wood, vicar, William Fisner, C. L. Beans, agent for John Cavendish Orred. There had been forty to fifty ques- tions addressed;to the applicants and answered and one of his special duties was to direct the attention of the meeting to these questions and answers. Mr. Russell then read these which showed that the heath, by ordnance survey had an area of 21 acres and the bill an area of 57 odd acres. The whole of the land lay to- gether. It was waste uncultivated land and consisted of sandstone rock, marl and peat turf. The heath was 219 felt and Beacon hill 260 feet above the level of ths sea. There were stone quarries on Runcoru Hileworked by lessees. The heath was a bed of marl and sandstone of doubtful q may. The whole land was waste of the Manor of Runcorn and the Lord of the Manor was the Marquis of Cholmondelev. Frephelders had. common rights ; there were no cripyholders. It was propcsed to leave the rights of the common as they now existed. Mr. Thomas F ard (Hill Farm) said he claimed a right of road across the heath and if the Council enclosed the heath what was to become of his road? Would they fence it off ? Mr. Russell said he would Come to roads presently and proceeded to read the rights of mieerals that were ves' ed in the Lord of the Manor,which as regarded Run- corn heath were to be abolished. That was the Lord of the Manor give up his rights to take gravel er stone from Runcorn Heath, or to interfere with the aoil there. His rights would remain as regarded Runcorn hill. In consideration of this concession the Lord of the Mancr would be given a small strip of land at the end of Mough- land-road cut off by straightening the road there,and the Council undertook to maintain the road to the boundary of the parish. The Council desired control of the hill to Prevent the deposit of rubbish,the unproper use of it by gipsiee, squatters. and others. It was desired to drain, manure and level the heath, to plant trees on the heath and hill,subject to the rights of the Lord of the Manor. There was no other ground available in the township for recreation. The inhabitants now resorted to the heath for recreation and games. Besides free access to the heath and hill, the Council desired the preservation of the eminence known as Beacon rock. The heath was to be devoted to cricket and football. Mr. Lake said the hill was not suitable for morsel° n at all. Mr. Russell said the next question was" Is it desired that carriage roads, bridle paths and footpaths over the common be set out in such directions as may appear commodious." The reply was: "The roads over the heath to be IS as all...avn on plan!, Mr. Russell asked if the roads as they existed at present were to be altered. Mr. Ford raid he wished to drive his cattle across the heath as his forefathers had done for over a hundred years. Mr. Linaker said he doubted whether Mr. Ford had any actual right to drive his cattle across. There was no road shown on the old maps. Mr. Lake: We don't propose to make any alterati in existing roads. Mr. Ford said if the Council laid out the heath as pleasure grounds and kept them in order would they protect the roads so that his cettle would not rue over the grounds. Mr. Lake: It will be our loss not yours, if the do. Mr. Russell said he could not say what tile Urban Council might do in the future. They might dony his (Mr. Ford's) right to the road. Mr. Ford : 'That is what I waist to know. Mr. Russell I must direct you to them" for an answer. Mr. Lake : I is not our present intention to interfere with the road. What the future will do Ido not know. (To Mr. Ford) You can come on the District Council and have a Voice in it. Mr. Ford remarked that it was a cindered road. Mr. Lake said the Waterworks Company cindered the road. Mr. Ford contended that it was cindered before the Waterworks came into existence. His family had farmed the land lor over a hundred years. Mr. Lake pointed out that the Council for access to the waterworks were bound to have a road somewhere about that place. mraissee observed that the Marquis of Cholrereseeley might quarry up the road and Mr. Ford could not stop him. Mr. Ford said he had used the road twelve years, and his forefathers before him, awl they could not step him. Mr. Russell : I don't think that is sound, you Isiiew. In reply to Mr. Berker,Mr. Ford said he was a yearly tenant under Major Orred. Mr. Ressdi You have waning to do wit% the Mar- eu s of Cholmondeley ? Mr. Ford : Nothing whatever. Mr. Raison pointed out that the Marquis of Chol- monde!ey's rights commenced lone before Mr. Ford's. Mr. Ford : Quite so. I understand that. Mr. Russian said Mr. Lake stated that it was not in- tended to inferfere with the roads at present, but he did net give any undertaking as to the future. Mr. Ford : That is what I want to get at. Mr. Rhssell : You must get elected to the Councieend have it all your own way. Mr. Ford : Quite so. (A. laugh) Proceeding with the, questions and answers,Mr.Russell said the lord of the manor was the only owner of the soil. There were about 20 persons entitled to riehts of common and pasture, and the lord of the Common and all the commoners had assented to the application. The assenting parties represented nearly the entire interest in the common. Mr. Russell now asked for the views of persons not pecuniarily interested. Mr. J. W. Lightburn said he lived at Heath House adjacent to the heath, and he Tate agreed with the application. Fie thought it would be a very groat im- provement indeed. Mr. W. Handley said for 30 years to his knowledge this question had arisen at public meetings, and had met with the approval of the inhabitants who had long wished that the time would dawn when the town would control the heath for the benefit of the neighbourhood. Deputations had waited upon the Council requesting ' them to take steps to acquire the common. It had been an election cry ever since he was a boy, Mr. Russell now examined the maps to ascertain the boundaries of the heLth and hill, and afterwards he ex- plained that certain portions were not now shown as belonging to the heath and hill which appeared on the tithe map as included in the heath and hill. He had gone through the portions referred to with Mr. Linaker, Mr. Wilding, Mr. Handley, and Mr. Ford and explana- tions which seemed to him satisfactory were given. For instance, a great many of the portions were very small pieces which had been annexed for such a number of years that it would be utterly hopeless to try to recover them, even if it were worth while doing so. Other por- tions were little tiny strips about nine inches wide, alongside some of the roads, probably strictly belonging to the common, but which would be of no use for the purpose of adding to it. There was land which for- merly belonged to the common which the Lord of the Manor gave up for the purposes of the waterworks and the hospital. There was one portion not included which however would be. Mr. Lig,htburn asked if the Council ceuld prevent football and cricket by bye-law if they found it neces- sary. Mr. Russell said he would not like to give a definite answer on the subject, but in his opinion the powers given be the conservators would enable them to control games just the same as everything else. Proceeding, Mr. Russell said it was stated in the answers that the control of the heath by the Council would improve the value of the adjoining land, rendering it available fur building sites instead of agriculture. It was proposed that the expenses incidental to the regulation uf the heath should be raised wholly or partly by the sale of a proportion of the common. The Board of Agriculture observed Mr. Russell, considered that as the expenses would be defrayed by the Urban Council there was no necessity for the sale of a part of the common, and such sale did not seem to be expedient. Mr. Lake said the expenses would have to be paid out of the rates otherwise, and the Council considered it ex-

pedient to save the rates. Mr. Russell : If you did sell these portions would they fetch sufficient money to serve the purpose intended P Mr. Lake replied that they had had an off .r of £4O for the piece at the bottom of the tennis field and a piece of land in exchange for improving the road. Thr,re bad been no offer for the other portion, a narrow strip. Mr. Russell said he wonld examine both pieces and tell the Board what he thought about the matter. In reply to Mr. Lake,Mr.Russell said the Council must apply to the Local Government Board in the ordinary way' for power to borrow money for the purpose of draining, setting out, &1., of the heath. Mr. Russell,accompanied by Messrs. Linaker.Handley, Lake and Wilding now drove to the heath and made a cursory survey, various points under discussion being pointed out. Mr. Russell held a second meeting in the small ball of the Public Hall en Wednesday evening. There were present Messrs. F. A. Lake (for the Council) C. E.

Linaker (for the Marquis of Cholmondelsy) J. Pritchard, W. Collier, J. W. Lightburn,W. Handley, J. F. Moulton, T. Bo 9 ,vver T. Watson, R. W. Harrop, Thomas Griffiths, J. Turrier,R. Lewis, F. L. Scott, E. Marshall (secretary to the Council), J. Wilding (survey ,r), and M. Barker consulting surveyor). He gave an opportunity to those present to object to the proposed scheme but no person raised the sliehtest opposition and Mr. Russell said he would report to that effect to the Board of Agriculture. In regard to borrowing powers mentioned by Mr. Lake at the inquiry on the previous day, Mr. Russell said he would include the suggestion in his report to the Board. The meeting then terminated.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

Thirty-eight points to lovA in a fortnight ! Such is the proud record ot the Warrington Rugby ootbslt Club, headquarters:Wilderspool,Warrington,Lancashire. Please make no mistake about the identity of the clo.a. Distinction is uot so easily attained that we would allow the possibility of any misunderstanding as to identity. Warrington have been moving along at a jog-trot for some years, but they have at last come out of their shell and astonished their supfyirters, themselves, and the football world generally. After this flash in the pan I suppose we shall have to re-accustom ourselves to the ordinary humdrum course of events. It is a pity. An occasional feat like last Saturday's stirs up one's blood and arouses our enthusiasm for such self-sacrifice. Not many, lambs went to the altar with such resigna- tion and meekness as did Warrington.

The altar in question was one supplied by tbe Hull Kingston Rovers,—good caterers for these articles. by the way. I heard some funny things said between 4-15 p.m. and 5 o'clock on Stturday. Once I just caught the remark " rhey're a better team than Warrington." Did you ever hear such a thing P Imagine any sane person saying that.

Perhaps I ought here to mention that the Hull Kings- ton Rovers above mentioned filled an open date which the Warri4gton club had for February 12th. They filled it well, and likewise Warrington's cup of bitterness, which now o'erflows. The Rovers have no locus standi in Yorkshire, they are in no League ; quite an insignifi- cant club in fact, but their existence has been sadly realised by a few Northern Union crack organisations lately. They came, saw, and conquered at Warrington. A committee man told me a week last Sunday that the Rovers were not the club now they once were, and that Warrington would beat them easily. He saw the Wilderspool entertainment on Saturday, and I trust he enjoyed it. It is not often one sees such a steady me- thodical procession to the goal•line as on Saturday.

But to come down to bard facts. Warrington had got a strong team on for the occasion, Barber regaining, Wilson,of Goole figuring on the wing, and the sturdy Naylor at half back. The Rovers went one better how- ever. Out of their batch of eleven County and Int:A-- national players they brought nine. Warrington might have survived that fact alone perhaps, but when these football luminaries turned out to be about 13 stone per man and 6 feet in height, things took a different com- plexion. The contrast when the teams lined out was amusing. Warrington are about the lightest of the Northern Union Senior Clubs and the R)vers considered to be the heaviest lot in England.

For the first five minutes of the game the homesl-ers appeared likely to make things warm for their -isiters, but never after did they have a look in or appear the least likely to score. The burly Rovers' forwards, who really look like a different race of men altogether, shoved Warrington this way and that. Boscow commenced by a little preliminary fooling, to encourage them you know ! end never made a bigger mistake in his life. They scrimmaged on the home line and instantly a burly white-headed 'tyke ayswering to the name of Debaey, squeezed through, and as all clubs but Warrfirmon eta kick goals, a couple more points were soon added. The Rovers roved round for a spell, always poem or lees on the line, and presently Fawcett, the full back, varied the programme with a drop at goal which succeeded. See- leg that they had just a chance of winning, the Rovers I forged ahead and chocking the ball over the heads of the I homesters in a tantalising fashion, it reached Tullocia who ran in at the corner. At half time Warrington were only a modest 10 points behind.

The second half found Warrington desp?rate and the Rovers jubilant. A few minutes pass od in skirmishing and then Fl.tcher, the half-back,plungsd over in a heap, no gAal resulting. Then a little more cavorting round and Kemp secured the oval, steam;ng over the line line an animated motor-car with a considerable portion of the home team hanging on to "the hem of his gar- ments." Again Guy made a guy of the goal kick. To ircrease Warrington's fun Barber retired hurt. The mighty Rovers' front rank finished the game with a grand bout of passing which ended in Lindsay taking a leisurely stroll behind the posts, Guy this time adding a goal. Thro3 goals, five trice to nothing was the sum total of Warrington's agony.

It is a difficult matter.; make a comparison between the teams, Warrington were so palpably outclassed. Not once during the whole game did Warrington really look dangerous, and the ease with which the Rovers piled on tries was remarkable in a meeting between two first class clubs. The visitors were nnmeasureably superior, the way in which their forwards worked the scrimmages and passed in the open being a treat the like of which has not been witnessed on the Warrington ground for years. I fancied that the Northern Union tendency to threequarter-play development would have militated against the success of a team relying on forward tactic., but the Rovers combined both so effectually that they proved irresistible. The front rank played a truly magnificent game, and were well supported behind by " Sammy " Morlitt, the two Guys, Tutioch, and

rawcrtt. On the home side Bate and Deakin were the only two men to a chowni v nfdorrmee the Captain'. exhibition beingresourcefulness Barber WAS a rlefi of s t a-or hai . s a toe plainly afraid of his knee to show much of his old dash, buten the other swing Wilson created a favourable impression. Though he only receiv?d two passes the whole afternoon he made good use of them, and showed cleverness in other ways. Naylor was only moderate at half back.

Hull Kingston Rovers hsse a rosy chance for the cup. In addition to their all-round strength, they have very easv matches in the first three rouuds.Hull Marlborough will be their first victims, Warrington St• Mary's or Fleetwood tho neat, and prob!blv Morley the third,arhile flunslet or Widnes will very likely meet them in round 4.

WARRINGTON OVERWHELMED AT

HOME.

The Warrington Football Club possessed an open date for last Saturday, and in order not to dis- appoint the crowd or decrease the Club's revenue, the Committee arranged a fixture with the Hull Kingston Rovers. The Rovers are new converts to Northern Unionism and at present have no place in the League, but they are the equals of any of the first division teams in Yorkshire. Their doughty deeds last season when in the Rugby Union made them interesting visitors to Warrington. As it was their first visit also, in the light of new foes they attracted much att snti on and there was a crowd of about 5,000 on the Wilders- pool ground to welcome them. On the home side Barber made his first appearance this year,and on the right wing. Wilson, of Goole, was tried for the first time. The home team was therefore a strong one and was expected to give the Yorkshire Rugby Union champions a putting up. These aspirations were soon dispelled, for in weight alone the Rovers possessed such an advan- tage that they were practically unconquerable. Warrington's attempts were very futile and the Revers succeeded in putting on a score of 21 points to nil. Mr. S. Williams (Salford) was the referee. Fine football weather favoured the encounter, and the ground was in good order. The players were as follows : Hull Kingston Hovers: Fawcett, full back ; S. Modtt, A. Guy, T. Ripton, and Tulloch, three- quarters ; W. Guy, and Fletcher,half backs; Rhodes, Kemp, Debney, Geenty, Duff, Stephenson, McDer- mott, and Lindsay,forwards. Warrington : Bescow, full back; Barber,Heesom, Isherwood and Wilson, three-quarters ; Bate and Naylor, half backs; Taylor, Deakin, Mason, Robey, Hill, Swift, Fairhurst, and Jolley, forwards. Taylor kicked off for Warrington against the wind and play settled at midfield. Tbe Rovers secured the oval

and punted to Boscow who dallied with the ball and lost ground. Heesom received from Bate but stuck to the ball instead of passing to Barber, who had a good open- ing. A kick from touch by Merlin carried play to the home line. Heesom dropping on the ball just as Her- fitt sprinted up. From the ensuing scrimmage, Bate tricked his opponents finely and ran to the centre before handing to Barber, who missed the pass. Fletcher got away from the Hull pack but Bate upset him promptly and thea Warrington entered the Hull halt where Naylor kicked badly in touch. A passing movement between Isherwood, Bate, and Heesoin came to nothing. Bate got away again and handed to Wilson who centred cleverly, Isherwood tackling W. Guy. A penalty was given against Hall and Mason had hard lines with the goal kick. Then the Rovers retaliated W. Guy making a fine effort and giving Ripton a pass on the line which was mulled. Mason and Deakin responded with a good run to centre, but the Hull forwards soon ruAied down the field again. Ripton was stopped in the corner by Wilson, and play again settled on the home line. Befare the Rovers could accomplish anything Bate cleared splendidly and kicked to Barber, Morfitt tackling the latter, and the ball went in touch. Morfitt centred and Isherwood saved near the posts. The next scrimmage saw Debney scoop up the ball and dive , over, A. Guy kicking a goal. Then Warrington livened up and dashed off to the other end, Barber punting strongly and Kemp just secured the ball before Morrison could race up. Bate attempted to dodge over but fell short of .the line, and Guy kicked out and prevented Boscow returning. Another fine run by Bate was nulli- fied by Kemp who intercepted his pass and renewed the attack. Isherwood put in a flying punt which Faw- cett received and dropped a particularly clever goal. Warrington had hardly established them- selves at the centre again before Rhodes, Debney, Lindsay, and Tulloch exchanged, the latter racing in at the corner. Guy failed at goal Warrington were now overplayed fore and aft and before the interval Morfitt Almost dodged his way over, and Tulloch missed drop- ping a goal by inches only. Deakin made a fine open- ing on one occassion but bad no one to pass to. Half- ' time, Rovers, 2 goals, 2 tries (10 poiats), ‘Varrington nil.

Rhodes set the ball in motices again an 4 after Naylor had returned strongly, Debney secured and being chal- lenged by Hill gave the latter a hand off which put him on his back. Warring,,too had just entered the Rovers' territory when a penalty kick drove them back,and from a scrimmage near the line Fletcher forced his way over. No goal resulted. Morlitt received from Fletcher but was grassed by Bather, and then a free kick to Warrington relieved tl-ran temporarily. The heavy Hull front rank soon recovered lost ground and gave the home backs no chance. A passing movement by Hull ended in Kemp being the fourth scorer, the big county forward carrying a omple of Warrins.,,tonians over with him. Guy again failed at goal. At this stage of the game Barber hurt his knee and left the field. ' With 14 men Warrington played desperately and managed to hold their opponents back for a time. I Wilson followed up smartly on two occasions, Fawcett I kicking dead to save his side. Deakin took the ball on the run and travelled half the length of the field before Fawcett pulled him down. Another bout of passing by the Rovers' forwards simply beat Warrington and Lind- say walked round the posts quite unopposed. A. Guy made no mistake with the goal. Warrington were plainly a thoroughly beaten team, and Tulloch made another fice dash for the line, Boscow checking his career. Time arrived with the Rovers in a majority of three goals, five tries (21 points) to Warrington nil.

Smart Dressmaking at molerate charges. SANSEY•STREEr, LEGH-STRE ET, WAR- INGION. UNIFICATION OF THE TOWNSHIPS OF WA.REINGTON

AND LATCIMI4D. Mr. Fred C. Halton, clerk of the Lancashire County Council, has issued the order asked for a short time ago by the Corporation " uniting the township of Latchfordlor all purposes to the town- ship of Warrington." The township of Warrington is to be included in and to form part of the War- rington Union. Every officer who, by virtue of the order, or of anything done in pursuance thereof, suffers any direct pecuniary loss by abolition of office or by diminution or loss of fees from salary is entitled to have compensation paid to him. Until a new valuation list is made for the township of Warrington the valuation list of the township of Latchford is to be deemed part of the valuation list of the township of Warrington. For the purposes of lists of persons entitled to vote at an election of members of Parliament, of lists of county and par- ochial electors, and of jury lists, the townships affected by the order are to continue to be deemed unaltered till the new lists are made. All recover- able arrears of rates due on the coming into opera- tion of this order in Latchford are to be recovered by the overseers of Warrington. The person holding the office of assistant overseer and collector of poor rates for the existing township of Latchford is to continue to bold that office. The order is to come into operation upon the day upon which it is con- firmed by the Local Government Board.

CASE AGAINST A FOR3IER RUNCORN SOBOOLMASTER.

SENT 'lO PENAL SERVITUDE.

On Tuesday in his charge to the Grand Jury at the Liverpool Assizes on Tussday,Ar. Justice Wills, the pre- siding judge, referred to the case of Thomas Cockshott formerly schoolinaster at Datton Workhouse, Runcorn, who was indicted on twelve counts. He said ammgst the general lists of ordinary crimes there was that of a man named Cacksbott, against whom there were many indictments, and the evidence in which showed as re- markable and ingenious a set of frauds as he had ever had to pay attention to. The prisoner was a well edu- cated man, apparently without anything known against him before, who had blossomed out suddenly into a criminal of a most famous degree. The grand jury would probably have no doubt that there was a prima facie csse against the prisoner. The case was heard on Wednesday. Thomas Cockshott, 28,sehoohnaster, was in- dicted on twelve collets charging him with various for- geries and misdemeanours committed in this city in September, October, and November last. He pleaded guilty. Mr. Tonin prosecuted on behalf of the Crown in regard to seve. al clf the indictments, and Mr. Pepe and Mr. Wh,srton appeared in support of others. Mr. Brad- shaw appeared on behalf of the accused.—ln briefly opening the case for the Crown, Mr. Tobin said that the 'felonies to which the prisoner had pleaded guilty con- sisted of forgeries, and the misdemeanours consisted of obtaining goods by false pretences. The evidence on the ' depositions showed that the prisoner had all his wits about him. The facts in the case that he should first instance occurred in September last. There was a firm ;in Birmingham known as Edwards and Barnes, who were in business as brass-founders, Mr. Barnes, a member of the firm, came to Liverpool, and prisoner, who was also at that time in Liverpool, found out by some means or other that there was a parcel lying at Lime-street Station, addressed to Mr. Barnes. He wrote a letter asking for the delivery of the parcel, signing it Barnes. Having thus obtained the parcel, be found in it a business card relating to the firm of Edwards and Barnes, and telegraphed to the firm in the name of Barnes asking for £lO to be forwarded, as he was short of money. Toe money was wired to him in accordance with that request, Counsel then described how prisoner obtained another sum of money by similar fraudulent pretences.—Mr. Pope and Mr. Wharton having also spoken with regard to the indict- ments against the prisoner, Mr. Bradshaw addressed his lordship in mitigation of the sentence,urghag, the respec- table character of the prisoner, and stating that some time ago he (prisoner) was an inmate of Whittingham Asylum, suffering from melancholia due to overstudy. Prisoner was set at liberty in six months, but had since been strange in his manner.—Evidence having been called on behalf of the prisoner,his Lordship said that be was sorry to have had to listen to the mischievous :tuff that had been brought forward for the defence. He said this on public grounds, because to have it supposed that because ,a person had fits when he was a child, and because nine years ago he was in a lunatic asylum—to have these things seriously brought forward as ground of mitigation of sentence was quite painful. He protested against being appealed to in that fashion, because it was mischievous for it to be supposed that any one could sit there and be swayed by any such con- sideration. Tbere was no ground for inia4:ining that prisoner did not know how to choose betweeii riehe and wrong, and in these frauds he had shown himself a per- son of keen intellect. The sentence of tCe court was that he must go to penal servitude for five years.

PENKETH.

FUNERAL OF Tile LATE MR. ROBERT GASKELL.—The funeral et the late Mr. Robert Gaskell took place at Great Sankey on Tuesday. The body was brought by road from Lee Hall, Prestbury. Toe relatives came by train, and met the hearse at Warrington, proceeding thence to Sankey. Several local gentlemen and the chil- dren attending the infant school at Penketh, founded by the MiBses Gaskell, awaited the arrival of the cortege in the churchyard. The mourners included Mr. W. Greg (son-in-law) and Mrs. Greg, Miss Gaskell (daughter), Mr. Francis Greg (coKsin), Mrs. Greg, the Misses H., M. and E. Greg, Master R. S. Greg, Mr. C. M. Halland,6lr. W. G. Holland, Miss Holland, Mr. G. Howson, and Mr. A. D.iscson (nephews), Mrs. Morton (niece). Amongst others present were noticed Mr. James Haynes, Mr. Henry Lee, Mr. Royal and Mr. W. Royal (Bath), Mr. W. Rogers (Weymouth), Mr. T. Birchall, Mr. R. Garnett, Mr. F. M)nas, Mr. and Mrs. W Long, the Rev. W. H. Drummond, and a number of servants. The service was impressively conducted by the Rev. E. S. Jackson (vicar), assisted by the R ,v. J. R. Jones (cure te),and the Rev. Mr. Roxburgh of Prestbury. The organist, Miss Standish p'ayed " 0 rest in the Lord " as the funeral party entered the church, and the Dead March in " Saul "at the close of the clese of the service. The . hymn "Thy will be done " was sung the choir assisting. At the graveside the choir sang " For ever with the Lord." The grave is the same in which Mrs. Gaskell was buried last October. It had been lined with green- ery and white flowers by Mr. Janson' the gardener at Lee Hall. The coffin was of polished oak with a brass plate bearing the inscription, " Robert Gaskell, born 30th December, 1814, died 10th February, 1898." There were a number of beautiful wreaths from the members of the family, the children of the infant school, Penketh and others. The whole of the funeral arrangements were carried out by Mr. C. Jackson Holmes.

PADGATE.

Dawn —A dance in connection with the Church Insti• tute was held in the schoolroom, on Friday evening last week. The room was nicely decorated with plants, kindly lent by Mr. Butlin. The dance was attended by about 80 ladies and gentlemen, the music (piano and violin) being supplied by Messrs. Finch and Clarke. Messrs. F. Blackwell and T. H. Granger, acted as rd. C's. The dance which was very enjoyable was kept up until about two o'clock the following morning, when the party dispersed.

The will of the late Sir Isaac Holden, Bart, of Oak- worth House, K,ighley, has been proved in the District Registry, at Wakefield. The net estate in the United Kingdom and abroad on which duty is payable has been sworn at £1,312,273. After payment of expenses, the residue is to be divided amongst his children, Sir Angus Holden, Bart., M.P., Mr. Edward Holden, Mr. Henry Illingwurth, and Mrs. Alfred Illingworth.

ONLY NINE O'CLOCK-NOT TWELVE

FOOTBALL

RUNCORN V. SWINTON. A GRAND DISPLAY.

The all conquering Swintonians we re the visitors at Runcorn on Saturday. They come with a splendid career of Buccesse3, and anticipated that they would add one more to them on Saturday. The home suppor- ters had not a particularly high opinion of the town's team before the game started, but later this opinion was greatly changed. Swinton brought a strong fifteen, J. At the Warrington Police Court on Thurslay morning, Mr. Wood, goods agent, London and North- Western Railway Company, applied to the magis- trates, Aldermen Sutton and BlackhArst, for their signatures to an sat tacit with respect to the "con- sumption" of 106 gallons of whiskey. In the acci- dent on the main line near Bank Quay on the 6th Met a cask of whiskey containing 106 gallons was burst and the contents wasted. The Inland Revenue authorities now require an affidavit that none of it was consumed, otherwise the Railway Company would have to pay duty on the 106 gallons to the enormous extent of 11l0.—Witnesses of the acci- dent swore that all the whiskey ran into the g'round,and the affidavit was signed and handed to Mr. Wood.

All the evidence now being collected goes far:to prove that Dr. TIBBLES' VI-COCOA has at- tained a popularity never before equalled in the history of any food-beverage or Cocoa. Since its introduction to public notice it has taken a firm hold, and its reputation among those who have once tried it has increased by the calumnies which have been hurled against it. Tradesmen are practically unanimous in their testimony of the large demand:for Dr. TIBBLES' TI-COCOA, as proved by their increasing sales, and the majority of them say that it:has obtained a place in public favour far in excess of any other cocoa which has yet been introduced. They attribute to the superior nature of the article itself, in that it is a food-beverage which:supplies stamina and strength to the body end recruits the faded energies of those who parcakelof it in a way that no other product of the kind attempts to do.

One gentleman (Capt. W. HOWLETT, Chief Officer and Secretary to the Jockey:Club, Owners, and Trainers' Fire Brigade, Fire Brigade OfficelWellington Street,Newmarket) hasrjust]written as follows :

"With great pleasure I send you this unsolicited testimonial. For thirty-two years I have been a member of Fire Brigades, and the last twelve years chief officer. I have tried :all sorts of cocoas, for often when called up in the middle of the :night to at- tend a fire one requires something to stimulate :and _stay the stomach, especially when getting soaked with water and choked with smoke and steam. Nothing I have tried has done me so much good as Dr. TIBBLES' VI-COCOA. It makes me fit for anything, however fatigued at or after a fire." In this age of competition and excitement, when the energies of mind rand body are so severely taxed, it comes as an ideal food, acceptable alike to the horny-handed iron-worker, the busy merchant, and the over-worked clerk. Not only the dwellers in the congested towns, but the sturdy farmer who gets =ore thani his share of fresh air, has found out the sustaining virtues of the beverage, and adds his contribution to the universal praise. From the opinions we have heard expressed, we are quite prepared to accept the statement that Dr.TIBBLES' VI COCOA combines the refreshing properties of tea, and the nourishment of pure cocoa, with the tonic and recuperative power possessed by neither, while it can be useninimany cases where tea and coffee are prohibited. Nobody takes it as a medicine, but it is accepted as a unique and wonderful food-beverage. Its virtue is derived from the African Kola nut, which is noted for its concentrated powers of nutriment, and its usefulness:ln enabling those who use it to undergo great physical exertion and fatigue.

Dr. TIBBLES' 'VI-COCOA has become a household word in all parts of the United Kingdom. This wonderful food-beverage has come to take an important place in the dietary of the best- regulated families ad has C 3310 to stay. Dr. TIBBLES' VI-COCOA is a natural food, and by It. merit alone—having been once fully and fairly placed before the public—it must become a national food, to the general advancement of British; health and vigour. It is in no sense a medicine (which too often aims at alleviating illness or curing disease), but Dr. TIBBLES' Vl- - has for its object the more rational one of strengthening the system and enabling it to resist, oppose and overcome attacks of,disease ; imparting a full capacity to enjoy every pleasure —moral, physicalad intellectual—by securing the proper assimilation of food, by which means only can be replaced the daily waste of tissue going oa within Us, and thereby helps us to en- counter with greater powers of endurance and more sustained energy the battle of life which day by day, in this age of rush and hurry, becomes more severe. Tired men, whether suffering from physical or mental fatigue, delicate women, growing girls, and little children find this unique beverage of inestimable benefit. The nauseous drugs and disagreeable medicines asso- ciated with ill-health are in VI-COCOA replaced by something which is pleasant and nice, with a distinctive flavouring of it, own, which will appeal to the faatidious and over-nice palate of the invalid, and its soothing effect renders it an ideal "Pick-me-up." Dr. TIBBLES' VI-COCOA, by taking the place of stimulants of an ardent nature, has made happy many a home which otherwise to-day would have been wretched, and the ultimate effects of which it is impossible to foresee. For breakfast, dinner, supper—indeed, at any hour of the tweuty-four—Vl-COCOA will be found light and refreshing; it leads the way among all beverages, and nothing can equal it.

THE NEW POLICE BUILDINGS AT

WA RRINGTON.

===== 0002601_18980218_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
A SITE SI4,LEUTED.

There was a special meeting of the Town Council on Tuesday evening in the Council Chamber at the Town Flail for the express purpose of selecting a site for the proposed new police buildings at War- rington.The proceedings were conducted in private, The Deputy-Mayor (Alderman James Fairclough) presided, and there was a good attendance of mem- bers. Quite a number of sites were suggested. We understand that Councillor Burton proposed the piece of land between Alderman Sutton's salerooms and the Baths in Legh-street ; Alderman Harrison thought the best site was the old County Court; Alderman Francomb suggested the site of the Liberal Club; Councillor Smethurst gave preference to the site in King-street, near Messrs. Walker's brewery, with an opening into Golborne-street. Councillor Tinnion argued in favour of the Town- hill site, and pointed out that the Corporation contemplated the widening of Horsemarket-street, Buttermarket-st., and Scotland-road. By building the station on this site they would do away with old and undesirable property, and moreover, the building would be in the most central part of the town. The place would be easy of access from all sides.

Councillor H. ROBERTS moved a resolation to the effect that the old militia barracks in Arpley-street be selected, becausa, in his opinion, it was the only site which filled the requirements of the Home Office. This was seconded by Councillor Dr. Connell.

After a long discussion the site of the old barracks was selected by fourteen votes to five. A resolution was passed directing the Borough Surveyor to issue directions to architects and invit- ing competitive designs for the proposed new build- ings, the cost of the buildings, exclusive of the site not to exceed .C 15,000. An attempt will be made to utilize the present buildings as far as practicable. The proposal will come ;up for formal ratification at the next ordinary meeting of the Council. In con- junction with the scheme there will be several sub- stations in and on the outlying portions of the borough.

BREWERS AND THEIR TENANTS.

IMPORTANT ACTION OF CREWE MAGISTRATES.

At Crewe on Tuesday Mr.T.Lathom applied,on behalf of Albert Tweedale, for a temporary authority to sell at the George and Dragon Hotel, MiU-st., one of Messrs. Walker's houses. This was the house which was shut up on account of the rrtiring tenatat,William ers,after he had received notice to q,.it from Messrs. Walker, re- fusing to part with his license. In the meantime Messrs. Walker had an anged terms with Byers, agreeing to pay bun between £l2O and £159, and now Byers attended and said he was satit-ficd, and surrsndered the license Tweedale produced his agreement. The Chairman of the Bench (Dr. Hodgson) : This is a different agreement as to the list of prices for liquors from that which Byers had. Mr. Latham : It is exactly the same' with the excep- tion that the cheaper beers have been left off. The Chairman: I see you will not allow this tenant to have any liquor .under 483. per barrel.

Mr. Lathins There is the discount. The Chairman : But it has been proved that the last man could not make a livinc, there at these prices. The applicant said he had been a railway guard. He had had no previous experience of the trade.

The Chairman: ilave you paid anything to go into this house ? Applicant: I paid a deposit. Hon, much: £lOO. What is that for ?—lt is for security for the stock and the license.

The Chairman : The license ! The brewers have no- thing to do with the license. The license, if yoq get it, belongs to you. We don't give •Messrs. Walker the license ; we give it to the tenant. Mr. Latham said he believed a deposit was paid in this way in almost every town except Crewe. The Chairman: We cannot sea what right the firm has to have a deposit on the licence. The licence is granted t ) the man who sells the liquor, not to the awners of the house. The Bench (to the applicant) : Have you made an agreement as to this deposit ?—Yes. Have you got that agreement P—No. The Chairman : That's strange ; men go into these houses of Messrs. Walker's bound hand and foot ' • they produce an agreement to us, and we afterwards find out there is an agreement behind an agreement. Mr. Latham said that it was not an agreement, but a receipt. Byers, the late tenant, was called, and said he was satisfied ss ith the terms which had been arranged be- tween Messrs. Walker and himself, and had surrendered the license. . . .

I Tweedale afterwards returned with his agreement, which was examined by the Bench. The Bench said that it was a receipt for security of license (which the firm had no right to have), and for rents and goods supplied. Tweedale said that so far as ha understood, it was for rent in advance. The Chairman : That is really a deposit which gives the brewers the claim over the tenant so as to enable them to retain the whole of this money when he leaves. Mr. Latham said he took it that the brewers would be entitled to keep that £lOO if the man lost his license by the improper conduct of the house. The same practice prevailed in other houses in the borough. The Chairman : I have no recollection of it. Mr. Latham said that if the bench desired it, he would strike out of the agreement or receipt—the words " security on license.' The Chairman : We do not like that agreement. We do not like that list of prices. Mr. Latham : But surely your worships do not want to interfere with the business terms of a firm and their tenants. . . .

The Chairman: We have had here a very experienced man, and he told us that those were terms on which he , could not carry on the business of the house and live. The magiFtrates were not satisfied at all. Mr. Latham submi•ted that the magistrates could net go into the terms of trade between Messrs. Walker and the tenant. The Chairman : But we have to see whether this man is a fit and proper person to be licensed to carry on the businese. We have it in evidence that the late tenant could not live at the prices quoted on his agreement. He was a man of long experience. The present applicant is new to the business ; he has the same price list ; he is bound down not to get liquors from anywhere else, and how can he carry on the business properly ? We are not satisfied. We have seen powerful brewery com- panies going and takieg shocking advantage of men within their power. In reply to the Benc's, Tweedals said he had no in- structions from the firm to give " long pull." He could give "long pull " if he liked. He had 6s. a barrel discount. After retiring, the Bench e :id that, while they had decided under the circumstances to grant the temporary authority,at the same time they had a strong disinclina- tion to do so. The case was this :—Messre. Walker bound their tenants down to take all their liquors from them, and yet they limited them as to the price of the liquors they should sell. That was, the tenant was not allowed to sell the quality of liquors that his customers might call for. That certainly was not the free use of the license in the public interest. The whole of the pro.; fits in that way were taken by the brewers, and none went to the tenant. The temporary authority would be granted until the next transfer sessions when the whole question would be gone into.

WARRINGTON SAILING CLUB,

Great interest is now being centred in the pro- gramme of the Warrington Sailing Club, and it is fully expected that during the ensuing summer racing will be indulged in on the stretch of water from Howley to Hobby's Locke. Knowles J. Chappel and F. Garnett, and T. and J. Jefferson. Two boats are being built in Warrington for Messrs. R. Bertham and H. Bertharn and Mr. Fair Turner. Practice will commence early in April.

THE USEFULNESS OF PAIN.

--- It sounds ridiculous to say that pain is an advantage to the world rather than otherwise Yet it is a fact. Not only is pain distinctly and beneficently useful, but if the faculty of feeling pain could be miraculously re- moved from the human body, with no other change in the organism, our race would inevitably became extinct in a very short time. Pain is a warning. The child that stretches out its baby fingers to"the pretty light" of a candle would Bowe day reach the candle or fire, and burn its unfeeling flesh to cinders. The pain of a knife prick saves us frooa wounds that would often be dangerous--the hand is in- stinctively drawn away with swiftness at the first sting. Watchful, beneficent, kindly, warning pain saves the life of every one of us many times in every year. Who shall say it is not useful ? _

_ . Half the times one suffers pain, paia is a message, easily understood by the thoughtful. After dinner, for instance, a sudden pain in the stomach, another pain la the head, a chest-ache, and a sensation (so uncomfort- able as to be practically pain also) of being too futile the waist—these things, taken together, convey a clear message : Your digestion is out of order. -The Past sort of pain—the full-waist sensation—often comesjover and over again before its useful message is reeognised: There was a CM-fully verified—in the papers lately,o2 a man vho was positively afraid to eat, owing co the pains of indigestion. This was Mr. John William:Spink a mill employe, of 10, Providence-street, Batley, linear Leeds, wed-known locally as a terrible sufferer. "I formerly suffered," said he, "with terrible pains in my stomach. If I took the least solid food I was torn with pain. At last my food was confined to milk and tapieca. I lost two stone in weight. I felt I could lie down and die. When I had had my dinner I went through agony. Now, however, I can eat anything and suffer ne discomfort at all. It's the talk of the neigh- bourhood and of all at the mill." Mrs. Spink here explained. "One day my brother said, • Why doesn't Jack try Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People ? They cured me of the same complaint.' I went straight away and bought a box. It is a remark- able thing to say, but the pains ciaappeared at!the first box. Mr. Spink cook five boxes in all, and is now sound and hearty, instead of a hopeless sufferer." RHEUMATIC PAINS ARE USEFUL also. But for them people would expose themselves to risks of colds which would probably prove fatal, leading to rheumatic lever, and thence to the disease of the heart which nearly always follows rheumatic fever, and eventually causes death. The twinges of rheumatism make people careful. Pain useful again ! There is a Mr. Haigh, living at 53, Milton-avenue, East Ham, Loudon, who would probably be dead now, instead of wetland flsurishiug, if rheumatism were painless. He was sixty-five lately, and liable, of course, to the pie:faiths of his age, haying suffered from rheumatism for years, and had also bad that deadly disease, rheumatic fever, which leaves heart disease be- hind it. In October, 1896, he caught a severe cold, ac- companied by a cough, followed by a return of his old malady, rheumatism. The first attack seems to have been similar to those which he had experienced before, the pain being confined to his limbs. Then it settled Its his back; "and." said Mr. Haigh, " I never remember having anything like it. The pain was at times so severe that I was forced to call out ; I was scarcely able to do anything but crawl into my office, which ii at the back of this house. I continued to get worse, and thought it was a case with me. I saw that something must be done, and that very slots. I had heard of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, and obtained a box. Knowing that my wife would laugh at me fur trying a fresh medicine, I went into the -office and took one of the Pills there. I-a an hour's tins I felt a little better : could stand straight up and do my w• riting I continued to take the Pills, and the next gone out of my back ; in fact, I felt was far as my case is concerned the Pine dever.ai well theae pain as se were magical. I have not had any rheumatism since. although this is the time of the year when I am troubled with it; the Pills warded it off."

Mr. Haigh trouble, which they cured, was of a kind that almost every man of his age suffers from, and Dr. Williams' Pink Pills have repeatedly cured cases width nothing else appeared to benefit. THE PARR HALL POPULAR CONCERT

Saturday night's concert in Parr hail, Warring- ton, was an unqualified success from all points of view. The attendance was large,though not over- whelmingly so, and the artistes ohe and all ac- quitted themselves in such style as to give satisfac- tion to all present. Perhaps the name which the Probyn Ladies' Orchestra has won for itself was the cause of the attendance of the majority present,but Mr. Nelson Jackson also has a large following, and the two combined can be relied on to draw at any time. Madame Edith Parker (contralto) and Mr.R. Wynne Jones (basso) were the other artistes. The Probyn Orchestra has already been adequately ens ionised in these columns, and we can only say that again on this occasion it was very highly apprecia- ted. This band of talented lady instrumentalists has so many perfections that faults are invisible,msd the most striking difference in its playing to that of similar orchestras is in the volume of tone produced. The programme was opened with a couple of orchestral selections " Der Rekrut " (Strauss) and the overture to " Maritana," both of which were finished performances, the lat- ter being especially well executed. Later in the overture to "Guillaume Tell," the capabilities of the orchestra were displayed to the best advantage, i while pleasing variety was found n the descriptive pieces "Travelling Through Europe," and " Hunt-. ing Scene." Miss Carrie Probyn, the lady cornetist, interpreted the difficult operatic air " Robert, toi que j'aime " with such expressiveness and ability as to constrain the audience to demand an encore. It is a further tribute to her powers that she Wall able to respond at once with a beautiful rendering of " Take a Pair of Sparkling Eyes." A couple of ' violin solos by Miss Edith Probyn stamped her as a finished executant, and Miss Gertie Probyn intro- ' duced a pleasing zylophone solo which was also a clever performance. The rich and musical basso of Mr. Wynne Jones earned him warm plaudits. His best effort was "The Ould Plaid Shawl," which to- gether with "True Till Death," as an encore, was rendered very finely. Isladame Edit la Parr achieved fair success, but neither of her songs in- fluenced the audience to demand an encore. The time allotted to Mr. Nelson Jackson was utilised by that gentleman with a mirth-provoking effect. His sketches and songs were of the clever and catchy variety Warrington people have learned to expect from him, and as each item was encored, there aux he no qualification of Mr. Jackson's success as a humorist. During the concert Miss Sophie Roberts, L.R.A.SI. officiated as accompanist.

THE DISTRESS IN THE WEST OF

IRELAND.

MEETING OF THE WARRINGTON COMMITTEE.

A meeting of the local committee ot the West of Ireland Distress Fund was held at the Town Hall last Friday afternoon. In the unavoidablelabsence of the Mayor, Mr. F. Monks, J.P., C.C., was voted to the chair. There were also present Councillors H. Roberts, John Crostleld and A. Bennett, Dr. Fox,Mr. H. Thornton, Mr. J. Lyon Whittle (town clerk) and Mr. Stanley Wilson (deputy town clerk).

THE BALANCE SHEET. The CHAIRMAN said the business of the meeting was to receive a statement of receipts and expendi- ture in connection with the distress fund, and to close the fund. The balance sheet showed that the cash received from subscriptions as per the adver- tised lists was £302 16s. 4d. Of this sum £278 3s. sd. had been paid to the Manchester Centre Fund, and £9 ss. had been paid direct. The ex- penses had absorbed the remainder. He moved the adoption ot the balance sheet. tCouncillor BENNETT : I suppose that is entirely in addition to the parcels of clothing which have been sent ? The TOWN CLERK : That is so. Quite £5O worth of clothing has been sent from Warrington.llll Councillor-RoBEnTs seconded the adoption of the financial statement, which was unanimously agreed to.

VOTES OF THINKS. The CHAIRMAN moved a votei of thauks to Dr. Fox for the initiative and the:interest he had taken in the matter. Councillor CRCSFIELD said be bad great pleasure in seconding the proposal. It was largely through Dr. Fox that the fund was started, and he thought it was to the credit of Warrington that the matter had been taken up in the manner it had been. The proposition was unanimously agreel to. Dr. Fox, in acknowledging the vote of thanks, said the good which he knew would result from their efforts amply repaid him for the trouble he had taken in the matter.lt had been a work of love to him,but he could not have carried it to such a successful issue had it not been for the assistance of the Mayor,the Town Clerk (Mr. J.Lyon Whittle), and the Assistant Town Clerk (Mr. Stanley Wilson). Mr. THORNTON moved a vote of thanks to the gentlemen mentioned by Dr. Fox. Councillor BENNETT seconded, and the resolution was carried. The Csananfax said those of them who had to do with the distress fund during the coal strike some years ago knew the value of the services of the ' Town Clerk, and his assistant, Mr. Wilson. The TOWN Cizas,in reply on behalf of bir.Wilson and himself said the work bad been a pleasure to them,and they were always glad to help any cause of that sort. (Hear, hear.) Councillor CROSFIELD alluded to the debate in the House of Commons on the subject of the dis- tress in Ireland. He said he was glad to see that the Government intended to set apart £lO,OOO for the purpose of relieving the distress. It would be spent in relief works. Dr. Fox said the Manchester fund amounted to about £4,000. He_learned that there was one gen- tleman who was appointed upon the Advisory Board in connection with the Dublin Distress Fund and who refused to act, because he said he was as- hamed that Dublin had not taken the initiative but had had to baled by Manchester. The proceedings then terminated.

THE HOPE FOUNDRY CO., LIMITED, WARRIN GTON. SOIREEJA/CD BALL.

There was a happy gathering in the Druids' Hall Sankey-street, last Friday, on the occasion of th first soiree and ball of the employs of the Hope Foundry Company, Limited. The directors and employes partook of a sumptuous repast, admir- ably catered for by Mr. Lazenby,whose indomitable zeal gave entire satisfaction to the participants. After the supper Mr. Joseph Titchett, one of the directors, took the chair and in a few well chosen remarks pointed out that although the firm was only in its infancy it was holding its own for enamelled and electro-plated ranges, etc., in Lan- cashire, Cheshire, and adjacent counties, and even as far as Scotland which fact was largely owing to the unanimity existing between the management and the workmen. Notwithstanding that the pre- mises had been considerably extended within the last six months,the supply was still inadequate to the demand for their goods,and further extensions would shortly have to be made. (Applause.) Mr. MARTIN FLANAGIIAN, in responding to the Chairman's remarks, very humorously remarked that although the firm was only young it had some very old heads at the helni,and it was satisfactory to know that the Hope Foundry Company's goods were cast, ground, enamelled and electro-plated at the works in Daliam-lane, and had not to be sent to different parts of the kingdom to be completed. During his remarks Mr. Flanaghan further men- tioned that the primary motive of having the soiree was to increase the friendship already existing be- tween the men themselves and the management, and for the purpose of forming a sick club in con- nection with the works so that when the bread winner was incapacitated through illness or death ample provision by this means would be made for the unfortunate family. (Applause.) Subsequently a programme of vocal and instru- mental music, interspersed with recitations &c., was gone through. Mr. Flanaghan gave a choice selection from " Maritime " on the clarionet. Mrs. Thomas Ince's recital of the heroic poem " The Spanish Champion" was highly appreciated by the audience, Her enunciation and pathog infused into the rendering of the piece was signal proof of her mastery of the art of elocution. Dir. James Fleming, in full highland costume, dis- coursed a grand selection of Scotch airs on the bag- pipes. Following the concert dancing was engaged in, and was kept up with zest until the early hours of this morning. Mr. Horace Wilks successfully discharged the duties of accompanist, and Mr. Wright supplied the music. Zvery credit is due to the committee consisting of Messrs. Martin Flanaghan,Harry Miller,W. Mitchell. Horace Wilks, and Thomas Inca for the excellent arrangements which prevailed.

WARRINGTON TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL.

TBE BOARD OF GUARDIANS' ELECTION.

The monthly meeting of this Council was held last week when there was a good attendance of delegates. After the routine business, the president (Mr. C. Morris) congratulated the Warrington Branch of the amalga- mated Saciety of Engineers upon rejoining the Council. He was pleased the difficulties in the way of a levy to the Trades Council had been removed to enable them to become affiliated again, and he trusted .they would be useful members in the future. A letter was received from Mr. S. Woods, M.P., re- gretting his inability, owing to numerous engagements, to attend a public meeting to be held shortly under the auspices of the Council. A communication was also read from the National Union of Shop Assistants containing a copy of the Shops Bill dratted by them and introduced into the House of Commons by Sir C. fluke. The special feature of the Bill which appeals most to shop assistants are the proposals for a uniform and earlier closing of shops and a limitation of the hours of labour to half an hour after the shop Is closed to the pablic, except on a certain specified number of days in the year. There is another Bil velure the House of Commons,known as Sir John Lubr ck's 13111,but it contains no proposal to limit the hour • a tradesman may employ his assistants after the shor is closed to the public end this at present is a real g ~evance with shop assistants. It was ultimately decid ed to endorse the Bill drafted by the Union. The nomination by the Council, of candidates for the forthcoming election of members of the Board of Guar- dians was taken into consideration,the present members Messrs. Lang and Malkin, for St. John and Bewsey wards respectively being requested to stand for re-elec- tion. Mr. Lang pointed out the difficulties that stand in his way of allowing himself to be nominated for the position,he not being able during the pest twelve months to fulfil the duties imposed upon him as the representa- tive of St. John Ward, and the Council expressed its , regret that there were obstacles in the way which pre- vented Mr. Lang taking up the nomination.—Mr. Malkin WBB somewhat surprised at the Council not sum- moning thereipresentatives of the respective wards give an account of their stewardship during the past three to years, and said he was prepared to do so at any future time. And if the Council thought fit to adopt him as their candidate he was quite willing to allow himself to be nominated, After a lengthy discussion, it was re- solved, en the proposition of Mr. Battereby, seconded by Mr. Nimmo, that both political parties be approached in the first instance with a view to a compromise being arranged in order to avoid a contest which would incur expense to parties interested and the ratepayers in general. And, in any event, Mr. Malkin, Mr. Delaney and Mr. Battersby were chosen as the candidates of the Council for nomination.

Sutton." H. ROBERTS.

BRUTAL ASSAULT BY AN IRISH LABOURER. Sir,—On the ttb instant an Irish labourer was brought up before the presiding magistrates—the Mayor and Mr. J. Flepherd—at the Warrington Police Court, and was sentenced to three months' hard labour for an assault on another labourer on the 3rd inst. Now, sir. I am one who desires that condign punishment should be meted our, to the delinquent, whether the crime committed be a misdemeanour, felony, or anything else. I hold then that the prisoner Fox received his, and ought ha he punished, brit to my mind it was more than adequate for the assault which he committed. There is one feature in connection with this incident—and mind I am not putting it forth as an extenuating circumstance —the prisoner was drunk. On the sworn evidence of the con- stable who lend charge of the case, and by the prisoner's ' own testimony, he got wet thrcugh and got some whiskey which overcame him. This poor but honest son of the soil has had to begin work perhaps at five o'clock in the morning and continue it until seven o'clock in the evening, and, perchance, being under a drenching rain most of these fourteen hours, coupled with the fact that he might have had an empty stomach—and we Irish labourers stomach's many a time have to be empty for the sake of our parents and little ones at home in our dear beautiful but miegoverned country, and for the purpose of staying the hand of an unsympathetic, cruel, and relentless evicting landlord. Consequently, his taking, whisky on this particular evening on an empty stomach was sufficient in itself to have its fell results. I am an abstainer myself, and would that abstinence from alcohol might permate the minds of all labourers and artizans of the British Isles. As I have previously said, no excuse should be taken as an extenuation for crime; still I think the very environments of the man, and the circumstances of the case—it was his first offence—ought to be considered. Before, however, the powers that be, had adjedieated the Chief Constable had to have hie ray in the matter. i His remark was as follows :—"The West of Ireland labourers were a blood-thirsty lot." Now Mr. Talbot that neservatinn i 3 incorrect. History proves it so. Voz pop:di is against you. On the contrary,we are the finest labourers, the hardest worked, the mast honest in Europe, and our employers confirm my statement. And last, though not least, the West of Ireland labourers will not COMP off seeond best physically, morally, socially and intellectually, with any other of their class. Sir, as an Irishman, I felt very much aggrieved at the remark of the Chief Constable. The West of Ireland and ite inhabitants are in moral attainments, ehoulders high over any ether portion of the British Empire.—erem,illegitimacy,decimal point four per thou- sand. I think in conclusion,the Chief of the Warrington police for his edification and enlightenment might do worse than spend his holidays in the West of Ireland,

MR. R. A. NAYLOR ON THE BIBLE AND IRISH DISTRESS.

Sir,—Mr. R. A. Naylor is reported as follows in your issue of the 12th inst. Speaking at an annual tea party of St. John's Presbyterian Church in this town he said : "They might have noticed the debate in the House of Commons on the distress in Ireland. There was one simple fact with reference to Ireland which would be in- teresting to them as a Christian community. They wbuld find that in Ireland where the people were mostly Pres- byterians and Protestants they were not in the condition complained of with reference to the others. That por- tion of Ireland which was inhabited principally by Pres- byterians was perhaps (sic) one of the most prosperous places in Great Britain." Mr. Naylor then goes on to say that " the Bible is the remedy for all the ills of na- tions and individuals." Suppose, without examining the direct cause of the distressed condition of the Catholics in the West of Ire- la.nd,and the alleged superiority of Protestant districts, we freely avow that the Protestants are in advance of the Catholics ; that they have adopted with more eager- ness, and carried out with more success, what may be called the utilitarian system,which in fact owes its ori- gin to the so-called Refermation. That they excel in commerce and speculation, in which they have greatly outwitted their more simple,perhaps,because more honest neighbours. That they also far excel in stock-jubbin,g. and are likewise adepts in all the mysteries of exchange. What then? Grant also if you will, that they surpass in banking, that they have issued many more notes" promising to pay," than the poor down- trodden turf cultivator, though the latter seldom fail to pay without any" promises " to that effect; nor have they ever been known to redeem their pledges by bank- ruptcy or renudiation—an easy modern—shall we add, Protestant ?—method of paying off old debts Let us acknowledge further, without fear, that Protestant countries have also published more books on political economy and the" wealth ot nations;" that they have also excelled in manufactures and machinery. _ . . .

But the modern utilitarian plan of conduaing the lat- ter, in England—land of glorious liberty and a free and open Bible—more particularly, has contributed not a little to impoverish and debase the lower orders of the people—which, however, according to the doctrine of the most fashionable theory, is not at all opposed to the " wealth of nations" ; for this is entirely compatible with the general poverty of the masses ! Suepose we grant all this ! What then ? If prosperity and success in business speculations' honest and otherwise • just or unjust—are the 'standards or true religion ; whet then kecomes of the Founder of Christianity ? Was He a promoter of Companies ? Was He a director of Building Societies? Was He a great landlord. Did He effect great Conquests? i e., rob people of their God given rights and freedom. Nay ! He was Poor, humble, meek, charitable, pure, despised, a male- factor. But in enlightenment of mhad,and in gentleness of manners, and in the general features and in the suavity of social intercourse, let us ask, do Protestant countries in Europe, really surpass Catholic Nations ? I think not. I believe the balance, if fairly poised, would rather incline in favour of the latter. It would be easy to show, that in point of general learning and enlightenment, Catholic countries compare most advantageously with those that are Protestant. This, I aver, can be established on unexceptionable Pro- testant authority. But has Mr. Naylor ever beard, that when Shakspeare was producing his immortal works in England, Mountjoy and Carew were reducing Ireland to the condition of a howling wilderness ? That the " Glorious Queen Bess" received from Ireland he com- forting assurance that nothing was left for her reign ever in that country but "carcases and ashes?" And later on, when Milton was giving to the world those splen- did cornpesPions which be knew that "posterity would not willingly let die," that Oliver Cromwell the puritan saint, with fire and sword was laying Ireland waste,and his captains were despatchiog the "Papists to Hell or Connaught ?" I; he so destitute of knowleig,e in Irish aff sirs that he does not know, that from time to time, under one pre- tence or other, the whsle surface of the country has been three times confiscated ? and, in the last change of owners, the principle of absolute personal property in the soil was introduced, which enabled the alien, finally settled in possession, to confiscate perennially all the the gains the labour of his serfs wrung from the land, that so atrocious has been the conduct of the Anglo- Norman landowner that his own Parliament has been compelled to declare him unworthy to exercise any longer the rights of proprietorship, and has reduced him to the condition of rent-charger or annuitant where he lately ruled with power as absolute and as cruel as a

===== 0002601_18980218_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY FEBRUARY 18. 1898

Turkish Pasha or an American slave owner ? Daes not Mr. Naylor know that for centuries the people °Lire- land have been subjected—and this by Bible Christians— to the most cruel and barbarous persecuting enactmeosts that ever disgraced the statute books of any land ? Yours truly, " SURSUM GORDA."
