POPULAR LEG PUR,E3 AT EARLESTOWN.

'TEL QUEST OF 'I HE PRPETUAL MOTION."

The second of the series of lectures given under the auspices of the Newton-in-Makerfield Lecture Committee was given in the Town Hall,Earlestown, on Thursday evening. The attendance was not nearly so large as on the occasion of the first lecture, doubtless owing to the inclemency of the weather, and the extreme scientific nature of the lecture which was given by Professor D. J. Carnegie, of Cambridge,on "The Quest of the Perpetual Motion." Dr. Hayward of Haydock presided, and said his first duty was to express to the Committee who had been concerned in arranging for this course of lec- tures his sense of the great honour they had done him in asking him to occupy the, position of chair- man that night. The duties of that position were not of a very arduous nature, lie really sometimes wondered whether it was not possible to have a lecture without a chairman. (Laughter.) He did not see that the position was one which was very useful, and on the present occasion it would hardly ' become him in any degree to confess that the chair- man was an ornamental appendage to the meeting. At any rats at least he thought he understood the exact duties of chairman on such an occasion as that. His duty was to formally introduce to them the lecturer. There had been occasions where the chairman had attempted to give the lecture,but he should not attempt that that night. lie then called upon Mr. D. J. Carnegie, M.A., to proceed with his lecture on " The Quest of the Perpetual Motion." (Applause.) . _ . .

. ... At the outset the Lect,urer stated that associated with all the older branches of knowledge, there lit I been problems which had excited profound iuterest even in those woo were outside admittedly scientific circles. These might be divided into two classes. Ibere were those problems which had no apparent or direct useful end in view. The old science of mathematics furnished Us with this class cf problem. As an illustration he might recall to their memory the time-honoured problem of squaring a circle. The problem Was to draw a square whiela would have the same area as A circle by geome- trical means. It was not simply to make a square equal to a circle but it must be done by geemetrieal means, All that they were alloWed in drawing the square were a piece of paper, a pencil, a straight- edged ruler,and a pair of compasses. (Laughter.) That was what they meant by geometrical means. The pro- blem seemed very simple, and it was its simplicity that had made for it its immortality. Mathematicians had become agree] that the problem was impossibleeand this conviction had crept into the speech of every day life. The second class of problems were those which had a distinctly useful end in view, and which when turned to a practical end in life proved an immense ho met not to the world at large, at least to the individual who happened to have solved it. There was the quest of the philosopher's stone that was to transform all the base metals into gold, and which if taken ietornally was to add to wealth, eternal health. (Laughter.) That left the alchemist with only the heavens ea strive for. (Laughter.) They had heard a great deal about the philosopher's stone. They had recently heard that gold had been made from Mein dollars. But they were aware that in the Mexican dollar all the gold had not bean extracted out of the silver and therefore a small amount of gold might be separated, but no more could be separated than there existed in the metel. He wished more particularly that night to give a short ac• ceunt, of the attempts which had been made to gain per- petual motion. The different motives of perpetual mo- tion seekers lead first to the distinction between honest investigators and impious frauds. (Laughter.) He would refer to one which came under the latter head. Some years ago in America a man named Readbefer in- vented whit he called a perpetual motion machine. Be means of diagrams which were shown upon the screen, the lecturer was able to clearly demonstrate to the audi- ence hew the invontor was able to cull a Gum- her of people by a machine which instead of haven r perpetual motion simply moved by clockwork concealed; in the basement. It was decided that the machine should be exhibited in the Franklin Insti- tute, Philadelphia, just to show the public how easily they could be gulled in a matter of this kind. He men• tioned that to show that they had to deal with frauds as well as honest men in this quest of the perpetual mo- tion. He would now confine his remarks to the motives of honest men,and they must now make one more classi- fication. It was not the same aim and object which presented itself to every investigator of perpetual mo- tion. While the msjority had the ideal of a machine which when once started would, without attention, and without the aid of natural forces such as wind, tide,sun- light, etc., do unlimited quantities of useful work, there was a minority who found sufficient spur in simply get- ting perpetual motion without any idea of getting work to be done by it. There were therefore these two sections of investigators—those who designed contrivances to create the power of doing useful work by virtue of self sustained movement, and those who wished ;to get mere Perpetual motion apart from all considerations of utility. In ascendance with this ilea it had been customary to distinguish the two by referring to the former as the perpetual motion, and to the latter as a perpetual motion. There was also another class who thought that the Government had offered a large sum of money for the discovery of perpetual motion. It was difficult to conceive how this came about, for nothing of the kind had been offered. The lecturer proceeded to dilate on the inventions of several perpetual motion seekers, and by copious illustrations the audience was led to more clearly understand the ideas and motives which prompted the inventore. In the first place, the lecturer dealt with contrivances founded on the partial know- ledge of the principle of moments. This principle. he explained, was that a given force when ;applied to a body which was capable of rotation was more effective in proportion as its point of application was further removed from the axis of rotation. Diagrams of Bossier's machine which endeavoured to make an over- balancing wheel work according to this principle were shown. A number of balls were arranged within the spokes of the wheel with the idea of giving it perpetual motion, but the lecturer pointed out how impossible it washy this means to give the effect which the inventor sought. The next contrivance referred to was that which was founded on the ignorance of what was known as the hydrostatic paradox, and which stated that the pressure of a fluid on the base of the vessel which con- tained it depended only on the area of the base and the depth of the fluid. The lecturer showed the fallacy of this contention, and explained that the pressure of a fluid was quite independent of the shape of a vessel —it being the same in all cases. Proceeding to refer to contrivances which were founded on a misapplication of Archimedes' principle that a body weighs less in water than in air by an amount equal to the weight of the water it displaces, he said that the Inventors took into account the buoyancy of a body un- der such conditions, but they forgot the thrust at the bottw.m part of the vessel. It therefore came about that the motion which the inventor sought' to gain by having more weight on one side of a chain working on a pulley in consequence of the other side runoing through water only Caused the chain to .vibrate backwards and for- wards until it came to the intermediate Fi at where it would weigh the same, and then it would atop. (Laugh- ter) Taking another type of machine, he referred to the contrivances founded on the misconception of so-called capillary phenomena. He said it was believed to ho absolutely true that liquids always tsund their own level, hut thie was not exactly correct. On investigation It was found that liquids did not stand at precisely their own levee but the difference was se small thet it was difficult to detect it with the naked eye.lnventors hoped that by taking advantage of tine breach of the general principle to successfully attack the stronghold of per- petual motion. (Laughter). The lecturer proceeded to refer to the machines which were invented with this ob- ject. and illustrated his remarks by diagrams of Sir William Crinereve's proposed machine. Another contriv- ance was founded on a misapprehension of the proper- ties of magnets, and this Was illustrated by the magnetic apparatus designed by Bishop Wilkins. It was shown that he force of the magnet which was used to draw a ball up an inclined plane also acted against the ball when going down the incline, and therefore made it useless as an agent of perpetual motion. There were also contri- vaeees Conceived in defiaece of the principle that what was saved in force WAS lost in distance. The uniform failure of all these attempts gradually forced home the truth in the minds of men that after all perpetual motion was a necessity from which men of genius could open noeloor of escape. The fundamental laws of modern science—the establishment of the laws of energy —once and for all laid low the spectre of perpetual motion. (Laughter.) The lawe of energy established that though the perpetual motion was un- conditionally impossible, e perpetual motion might he obtained if they encceeded in eliminating friction. It might he asked what were the credentials of the laws of mercy? If these laws were true they were able to make certain deductions from them which could be put to the experimental test. Many had been naade,and in no ease had such deductions been found to fail. The lecturer then referred to the experimental tests which had been made by James Thompson and Lord Kelvin which de- monstrated the truth of the laws of energy, and he also Raveseveral experiments himself with ice and india-rub- ber corroborating his cententlen. In conclusion he said one could not but regret that as much mechanical at- tention as was often shnwn should be lavishly sacrificed on the ratter of impossibility. He exhibited a slide ot a lever chain which was on the market at the preseht day. It was claimed en account of this leverage that there was an eeonenay in power. As a matter of fact there was no leverage at all. Besides if it were there it would not make any matter, it would not econemiee newer at all. because power consisted of two things which they must multiply tneether, force arid. distance, and they could not economise newer by increasing Isvarage.Tbese verdicts of the laws of energy only applied to objects which they could see an touch and handle, objects they could experiment with. The Perpetual Motion was ins.

SUNDAY OBSEAVANCE.

Sir.—l was pleased to see in your issue of February 12th that lona has been a reader of the Bible all his life, for I also am a daily student of that book, which I be- lieve to be (as originally given) wholly inspired, and therefore entirely to be relied on ; notwithstanding the hither critic and others named in Palma 14 and 53, verse 1. I infer:from some of the remarks in lope's last communication that the way he has " been brought up from childhcesd " is responsible for his failure to appre- ciate the points for which I have contended, rather than want of " critical acumen" on his part. Perhaps to a certain point,my experience would be not dissimilar to his. Brought up as a regular attendant at a place of wraship, I was taught to keep holy the first day of the week, and that, under certain circumstances, I should go to Heaven when I died, and under other con- ditions to Hell, to be tormented for ever. If any reader of the Bible will take either or both these last items as axioms when commencing or continuing the study of the Book, he will be exceedingly fortunate if he ever gets the true teaching of the Word ; and it is possibly in a reflex way on ac- count of some such or similar belief, that lona quote; Matthew 5 with reference to the law. The 17, 18 and 19 'Verses of that Chapter are singularly ill-chosen by your correspondent, as with your pernsission,Mr.Editer,l will endeavour to make clear. In the 17th verse Jesus Christ says He is come to fulfil the law, and that is precisely What He did in all points, for He was (oeing a Jew) amenable to the Mosaic law and had Ele not kept the law He would have been sinful and not sinlese, as we know He was, from the Word. The coratention of your corres- pondent—quite unintentionally I believe on his p art— makes Jesus a sinful man, howevet, for if his inference is true, as placed before your readers on the 12th inst., then one of three things must be admitted—either,firat, that He did not speak the truth ; or secoud,that heaven and earth had passed away ; or otherwise; third, that not one jot or one tittle has passed from the law. The first of these may be at once dis- missed as inadmissible, though Imes' conten- tion for the " perinanance ot the fourth Commandment as a jot or tittle of the law which shall last till Heaven and earth have passed away" would compel us to admit the proposition. But the jots and tittles have passed away, as well as those things apper- taining to the Mosaic Law which we might consider of more importance. Only one or two cases need be quoted to prove this, and we need not go outside the &ends Commandment for our first example. The observance of the seventh day as a day of rest in the strict sense en- joined in the Seriptures is a thing of the past among even those who do regard theseventh as a day to be observed in accordance with the Fourth Commandment. The morning and evening sacrifice is also a thing of the past; as also the numerous other injunctions contained in the law as regards feasts and fasts,the years of jubilee, to., &c., and therefore as Jesus Christ spoke the truth and as any candid reader must admit the jots and tittles of the law have passed away, we are left with the third proposition... That Heaven and earth have passed away." Your correspondent is apparently under the impression that the literal heaven and earth are referred to in Mat. 5. 18, evidently overlooking the fact that in the sth verse of the same chapter Jesus had taught his disciples that the meek shall inherit the earth, which agrees with the Psalmist's statements in the 37 chap. and verses 9, 11, 22. 29 and 34, as also the spin' in Revelation 5 and verse 10 which read; •' we shall reign on the earth." I cannot admit the reasonableness of a legacy being promised by Jesus to his disciples, which it was intended should be burned up at some future time, and on look- ing further into the matter we find Peter referring to the same heaven and earth which were to pass away with a great noise. As to take the heaven and earth here spoken of is a literal sense would contradict the Several passages before quoted, I am compelled to conclude they are used symbolically es is the case in such pass- ages as Ezekiel 32,7 and 8. Ise. 34, 4.10 and Zech. 1, xi, and we therefore submit that the heavens (or ruling powers) and the earth (or people ruled), that is the Mosaic heavens and earth, passed away finally at the fall ef Jerusalem before the Roman armies. I confess to a feeling of astonished amazement that any reader of the Bible should contend that the Mosaic 1 law will last till the literal heaven and earth have passed away, and am almost equally surprised that lona 1 is not able to see that we are now living under a differ- ent dispensation, seeing that there are five distinct periods, past, present, and future enumerated in the Bible,LeAntedilovian,Postdiluvian,Mosaic,Christian and Millenial. The gist of my contention is that we are now under the fourth or Christian period; and it would be as unreasonable to expect us to obey now the laws of the future Millenial period—one of which is „ thatsevery one shall go up to Jerusalem from year to year to worship the King, the Lorrlbf Heats, and to keep thaleset of Tabernacles (Zechariah 14 .6), as it is to expect us now to obey the Mosaic. - Gee word more before closing this letter. The third chapter of Galatians, at verse 19, telle lie that the law serves till the seed should came—not till 1893 years after —and at the 24th verse. Paul informs the Galatians that the law was—not is—their schoolmaster to bring then to Christ, i.e, to bring them down tithe time. of Christ, and at the 25sh verse making assuranse doubly sure he tells them they ore no longer under a schoolmaster i.e, the law. I think I may again refresh my Need "lona " by asserting emphatically that the Mosaic Law is ended,and while thinkine, him for tha manner in which he hae performed his part of the correspcmdence,would venture to hope that as a result he—and many other Bible readers who may have read the letters—may examine critM illy the belief they hold and see whether they are Bible doctrines or merely traditions of men,for the words are equally true and applicable now as when uttered Matthew 15. 9, " In vain do they worship me, teas bine for doctrines the commandments of men."—Yours truly, R. G. L.

RUNCORN FOOTBALL NOTES.

What a surprise it must have been to Northern Union enthusiasts when it became known that Runcorn had marred the progress of Swinton. Coming to Runcorn after they had inflicted such a crushing defeat on War- rington the previous Saturday, not many thought that the home men would be of much use against the all con- quering "lions." But the Runcorn 15 is composed of a set of men who play every Saturday as though they were confident tisat the match was theirs. As I said last week they would rise to the occasion seine day and this they did with a vengeance for they were the team who deserved to win.

Although Runcorn were th 3 best team they must not yet be satisfied, for the back division is very shaky and is easily split up. As soon as they drop on a good three- quarter set it would be well to look after tuem as the lesson of the past ten weeks has shown, that is redly what is wanted to make Runcorn one of the test teams in the Union.

The game on • Saturday was exceedingly interesting. The home forwards played like champions and worked together as one. The half backs were good but the three-quarters were very weak. liou4loton was a tower of streuTh and saved his side a lot of work. The three- quarter and half backs on Swinton's side were good but the forwards were nothing Co be compared with Run- corn. Valentine was too well marked to do anything and ad 90011 as he got the ball he was pounced upon. Goodman was fair.

CHILDRENS' CONCERT AT WARORAVE.

In connection with the Wargrave Day School a suc- cessful concert Teas given by the scholars in the .school- room on .Thursday and Friday evenings last week. On the opening night there was a crowded audience who thoroughly enjoyed the items submitted by the young- sters. The first part of the programme consisted of an action song" rho Visitors," whicliwas admirablyii endered by the Juniors, a dumb-bell by the infants which was remarkably well executed, and an action song " Topsy •rvY," which caused much amusement. For these .ems Miss Warburton and Miss Barlow were responsible, sad the efficient manner in which the children acquitted themselves reflected the greatest credit upon their tutors.

The second part ef the programme was a Kinderspiel entitled " Pie Happy Family," for which toe children had been trained by Mr. R. Chand- ley, bead master. Toe characters were :—The old woman who lived in a shoe (Mtge M. Holt), Aladdin (Master A. Taylor), the rnidshprnite (Master H. Sweot- love), Sinbad (Master P.Laithwaite),Blue Beard (Master It. Faulkner), Ali Baba (Master H. Large), Robinson Cruise (Master H. Shepherd), Friday (Master Graham Wright), the dog (Master William Lomas), Miss Muffett (Miss E. Gutteridge),Marjory Daw (Miss M. Sweetlove), Dorothy Drageletall (Miss G.Oubridge), Cinderella (Miss S.MolYsieux),Goody Two Shoes (Miss Polly Naylor). It is needless to follow the argument through, except to gay that the Old Woman finding the shoe too small for her numerous family had to take up a more commodi- ous resi lence at Green Elms Cottage, and with the ex- ception of two or three she was seen with her family gathered round her,the exception being Aladdin,Sinbad, Blue Beard, Robinson Crusoe, and AR Baba, who were supposed to be in distant lands. They, however,return at various intervals, and are duly welcomed by the old woman and the remainder of the family with song and dance. The costumes were very neat and appropriate, and the dialogues were capitally given by the children, the connective songs being also admirably rendered. The make-up of Man Friday and the Dog were especially good, and the antics of the dog created inteese amusement amongst the juvenile por- tion of the audience. The tug of war I:,ween Ali Baba's men and the sailors was followed by a hornpipe well executed. The Grand March and pro- cession of the principle characters was a pretty conclud- ing item, the lime-light, worked by Mr. Lawson, adding considerably to the effect, The concluding item was a dance by 12 children in costume,which was particularly effective,and gracefully executed. At the close of Friday's performance, the Rector, Rev, T. Carson, proposed a vote of thanks to those taking part, to which Mr. Chandley responded. The accimpatiists were Miss M. Sweetlove and Master H. Purnell. NEWTON URBAN Disrßicr COUNCIL v. 3IES3R.S. It. P.IVA.NS AND CO. CLAIM FOR cosrs.

GYMNASTIC COMPETITION AT WAR RIN GTON.

--- The attendance at the Warrington Gymnasium on Saturday evening when the first gymnastic com- petition of the season touk place was not so large and encouraging as the occasion demanded. With Mr. J. Kinsley devoting his whole time as director of exercises, ant backed up by an energetic com- mittee, the Gymnasium may now be said to be do- ing better work than at any previous time in its history. It was therefore somewhat disappointing that the hall was not completely filled with specta- tors to witness the contest betweeathe Warrington champion eight, and a like team from Winsford. The proceedings were presided over by Mr. D Jones, the Chairman of the 'committee,and in the course of a few introductory remarks he pointed out that the gymnasium was an institution for the special bene- fit of young people, and he hoped the young people of Warrington would make good use of it. (Ap- plause.) The competition was then proceeded with, the onerous duties of judge devolving upon Mr. T. Renshaw, of Manchester. The only alteration in the teams selected was the substitution of W. Leg- gatt for R. Bennett in the Winsford team. The first item was a performance on the rings,and in this the Warrington men showed their superiority. This re- mark applies more especially to A. Thomas, and A. Mottram, whose performances were a credit alike to themselves and the institution. The Winsford men were more clumsy in their movements and seemed to suffer from lack of practise. The total scores in this contest were Warrington 97, Winsford 81, thus giving the home team a lead of 19 points. In the dumb-bell exercise which followed Winsford almost regained the points they had lost in the previous exercises, by securing 145 points to their opponent's 132. In this department the Winsford men were undoubtedly the best, and they made very few slips as compared with the Warring- ton eight. Miss Clayton was the pianist. The con- chiding item was rope climbing. Seven points were awarded for once climbing to the top of the rope, and double the number of points the second time. Thomas was the best on the Warrington side scoring a meritorious 24 points, whilst for Winsford, Dean ran him close with 22 points. Altogether the War- rington team secured 124 points, and Winsford,ll3. The result of the competition was therefore a victory for Warrington by 14 points. The individual scores were as follows:

WINSFORD. Instructor : Mr. lIASIAIII. non. Sec. : Mr. W. Shaw. Team. Rings. Dumb-hello. Rope. W Legzatt 7 ... 12 W Stunner 6 ... 11 J Sumner ... W Neden H Ikin l3 ... 16 H Rigby l2 ... 14 W Hulse lO ... 16 11 Dean

Totals ' 81 145 WARBINGTON. . _

After the contest the Winsford team gave an ex- cellent exhibition of club exercises, and the War- rington men followed them with an equally clever display with the barbells. The Chairman's services were acknowledged on the proposition of Councillor Tinnion,who referred in eulogistic terms to the ser- vices of Mr. Jones.— Mr.Jones,in acknowledging the compliment, said he desired ta sea the Gymnasium in a flourishing condition.

UREAT SANKEY PA.RISII COUNCIL.

A meeting of thia Council was held in the Great Sankey Board Schools on Monday evening. Mr. f. Hal- ton presided, and there were also present Mr. W. Taylor, Mr. T. Cooke, with Mr. J. Smith (clerk). THE ALLEGED ENCROACHMENT AT SAE.

The Clerk reported that in accordance with instruc- tions received at the last meeting he wrote to Mr. Selby calling attention to the dangerous state of the fence at the junction of Long-lane and Liverpool-road, and stat- ing that the Council were of opinion that an encroach- ment had taken place there. He had received the fol- lowing reply :—" Leigh, near Manchester, 31st January, 1893. I have personally view the land to which you refer and have compared the present boundary with the old plan, and I am satisfied that there has been no encoachment as alleged by you. Under these circumstances I see no object in meeting the members of your Council but as a matter of courtesy if they still desire an interview I will arrange to meet them at the junction of Long-lane and Liver- pool-road at one o'clock on aay Wednesday. You should address your complaint as to the broken down fence to the occupiers, Messrs. Singleton and Stephenson. Your obedient servant, John Selby. Mr. J. Smith, clerk."— The Chairman said he thought it would be advisable to ask Mr. Selby to meet Mr. Thompson and himself next Wednesday week.—rhe Clerk said he Mgkt state that the fence had been temperarily repaired.—Mr. Cooke said be did not think an encroachment had taken place. Ile had known the place for a generation, and he did not notice any difference. 'lie was of opinion that the encrdachment was not worth bothering about. —The Chairman said the Council was entirely in the hands of Mr. Selby.—The Clerk said the fen e seemed to come to a poiat near the gateway and looked as if a slight encroachment had taken place.—lt was decided to leave the matter in the hands of Mr. Thompson and the Chairman who were to interview Mr. Selby on the sub- ject.

VIIBEI SCEOLAIVIRIPS. The Clerk said ha had seen the schoolmaster (Mr. Clarke) with, reference to the free scholarships at Faro- worth Grammar School which were open to boys be- longing to Great Sankey. He suggested that the Chair- man should take the matter up as a School Board matter and relieve the Council front it.—The Chairman said he would be most happy to bring the subject before the members of the School Board. THE LIGHTING OF VEHICLES. The Clerk said the communication from the Whistoia Parish Council asking- the Council to petition the Lancashire County Council in favour of the lighting of vehicles after sunset was allowed to remain over to that meeting. He pointed out that there was a byelaw in Cheshire carrying out the auggestion.—Mr. Taylor said it would be very dangerous for farmers when carting in- flammable material suet as hay, straw, etc.--Mr. Cooke said there had been no complaints heard in that district with reference to the non-lighting of vehicles. He sug- gested that the matter should be left over for the new Parish Council to express an opinion upon.—The Chair- man said there was no doubt that the maj trity of the , members of the present Parish Council were against the 1 proposal to light vehicles.—lt was decided to leave the matter over for the next Council.

TIE CURBING OF FOOTPATHS.

The Clerk staTd-iiir-.7ftiOnipTon-,-w—ri-o—w-tie. unable to be ' present that night, gave notice that he would move the following resolution : "That the attention of the Coun- cil be called to the footpath on the Southerly side of Liverpool-road being without curbs, and to the danger to pedestrians consequent upon cyclists being in a posi- tion to ride thereon."—The Chairman thought it was an unnecessary expense oa the parish to provide cur'os simply because cyclists rode on the parapets when curbs were not provided.—The Clerk said if the footpath was distinguishable from the road it ought to be called a footpath.—The Chairman said where there was a raised channel it was sufficient to show that there was a footpath. He did not agree with a recent decision of the Warriagton County magistrates where they refused to fine a cyclist bre sure there was no curb ta the footpath. —l4r. Taylor said he did not believe there were any footpaths provided with curbs for any great distance in the townehip.—The Clerk said if a cyclist were per- mitted to ride on such footpaths,drivers of vehicles would , begin to follow their example. Where there was a raised channel it was sufficient to show the difference between the road and tbe foetpath. He understood the police were anxious to prosecute the next cyclist they caught on such a footpath.—The Chairman said he learned that the recent decision of the Warrington County magis- trates in the case from that township was going to be appealed against by the police.—The subject then dropped. MATHEW —RIG3Y. I At St. Phillip Neri ([lie Oratory), Brompten-roadt Kensington, London, on Saturday .afternoon, the quie wedding took place of Mr. 'Theobald Mathew, eldest son of the lion. Sir James Charles Mathew, L.L.D.,who WAS appointed a judge of the High Court of Justice in 1881, chairman of the Irish Evicted Tenants Commission 1892 a member of the Legal Education Council, and chairman of the Board of Studies of Inns of Court, and grandson of the late Mr. Charles Mathew, of Lehena House.clunty Cork, and Miss Ruth Rigby, daughter of the late Rev. G. H. Rigby ,rector of Yardley Hastings, Northampton- shire, and niece of the Right lion. Sir John Rigby, Q.C., who was Selicitor-General in 1882, and M.P., for the North Division of Cambridgeshire in 1886, and afterwards for Forfarehire, of 16, Chelsea Embankment (Carlyle House), and Glenturret, Crieff, N. 8., and grand-daughter of the late Dr. Thomas Rigby, of Hal- ton, Cheshire. This marriage being what is termed in the Roman Catholic Church a" mixed ceremony "—the bridegroom being a Roman Catholic and the bride a Protestant—there was not a single note of music, but i the chapel of St. Wilfred, at the extreme right-hand side 'of the Oratory, where the marriage took place, was , handsomely decorated with palms and white flower& 1 The nuptial ceremony was conducted by the Rev. Father Cator, of the Oratory, assisted by other priests of the edifice. The bride was accompanied by her uncle, Lord Justice Rigby, who conducted her up the long aisle to the sanctuary, and . afterwards to St. Wilfred' Chapel, when in due course his lordship gave her away. Mr. Arthur Hunger. ford Potter, a personal friend of the bridegroom. was best man. There were neither bridesmaids nor pages. Miss Ruth Rigby looked exceedingly well in a very plain and neat weddiuggown of white chiffon over an under- gown and bodice of white satin hemmed with frills of chiffon ; epaulette aud neck of white mousseline de sole. Her tulle veil covered a tiara of orange flowers, and her only ornament was a massive gold and diamond brooch i (affixing the front of the dress), and she carried a huge ' bridal bouquet of white flowers in foliage tied with white satin streamers. Among those present at the Oratory to witness tha ceremony were the Hon. Lilian Russell, the Hon. Frank Russell (the Lard Chief Justice of England'e daugh- ter and son), the Hon. Malcolm Macnaughton son of Lord Macnaughton), Mr. John Diller', M.P., and Mrs. John Dillon(brother-ie-la w and sister of the bridegroom). T "Temple Maghzine " tells a good story in an in terview with Dr. Whipple, tho Bishop of Minnesota : Many years ago (says the Bishop, who is testifying, to the honesty of the Bed Indian) I was hiolding a service near an Indian village camp. My things were scattered about in a lodge,and when I was going out I asked the chief if it was safe to leave thorn there while I went to the village to hold a service. Yes,' he said, ' perfectly safe. Thero is not a white man within a hundred miles !' "

The alleged discovery of an entire rnamms)th, well preserved in ice, by M. Nociloff,a Russian explorer,has a familiar ring. A similar story reached the St. Petersburg Academy of Sciences in 1877. One Gromoff, a placer- miner in Siberia, alleged tnat he had come upon the remains of one of these colossal beasts,and the news was at once wired to the capital. Numerous telegrams were exchanged, and arrangements were entered into for con- veying the carcass whole from Atchinsk to the banks of the Neva. Presently a scientific commission arrived on the spot,when it turned out that ths supposed mammoth was a mere layer of an elastic mineral-titre talc, the flesh below consisting of the edible clay which is familiar to Siberian geologists. Since then Russian scientists have been rather sceptical as to prehistoric remains.

"There is nothing, comparatively speaking, culpable'. in an unmarried man flirting. The culpableness comes in when one of the parties is married—now, in your case—" •flush is ejaculated Mr. 'Hughes, looking aghast.. ."Hush ! " echoed his wife. "You really must think before you speak, Dennis. It is not comme il faut to talk like that. lam quite shocked at you —quite! " "But, aunt, you make such leading remarks. I can't help taking them up," replied Dennis, un- abashed. "Let us try and keep to the subject under discus- sion," said Mr. Hughes, drawing up his chair and speaking with studied seriousness. "In short, your aunt and I feel ourselves placed in a very painful position, Dennis. Mrs. Treherne asked me to exercise a religious protectorate—so to speak—over her daughters, to screen them from the society of young men, and to keep them out of harm generally." "And I am doing my level best to assist you in so doing," put in Dennis. "But not in a rammer of which I can approve. On the contrary, you are flirting disgracefully with the elder Miss Treherne !" said Mrs. Hunhes. "Miss TreliernJ!" ejaculated Dennis, with well- feigned surprise, veering round and facing his aunt with astonishment. "'When you referred to my flirting just now, I naturally imagined you meant my attentions to the stout French Baroness!" "The French Baroness! Oh, Dennis! She's forty-five at the very leastt, and she positively paints her face. She was out in the rain yesterday, without on umbrella, and the red paint ran off her cheeks down to her chin. The effect was most curious." "Maria., my dear, I wish you would keep to the subject under discussion, and not fly off at a tangent," expostulated Mr. Hughes. "We were talking aboid, ....ss. ssretierne just then. Now, Dennis," he continued, turning towards his nephew, "we have noticed that you talk to her, and remain in her proximity, in a way that—well, to be explicit, is neither seemly nor correct., and I must ask you to cease paying her attentions." "Attentions! Surely there is nothing conspicu- ously attentive i^ mv to Miss Treherne? I am merely polite and pleasant," replied • Dennis calmly. "Much too polite!" ejaculated his aunt. —_ "Far too -pleasant," added his uncle. "You must really desist from taking up your position at her side on every available occasion. It is not at all correct," continued his aunt. "But, uncle, I have noticed time. after time that you devote yourself with equal assiduity to Miss Treherne. 'Whenever I am at one side of her, it seems to me that you are on the other." "But lam simply acting as her escort. I must not neglect my duties or shirk responsibilities," explained Mr. Hughes. "Certainly .11... L. I :suppose it has never occurred to you that people often depute their duties to othera ? " said Dennis, adopt ing a tone of argument. "Now, for instance, to whom could you more judiciously depute your duties than to your trust- worthy nephew, Dennis Vane ? " ' "I ;In not s - • • .1- , lisht," answered Mr. Hughes, in a voice of surprise. "Most preposterous suggestion!" exclaimed his wife, indignantly, as she noticed a smile lurking round Dennis's mouth. "What I suggest is that you place a limit on the time you spend each day in Miss Trelierne's society," said Mr. Hughes, assuming a practical tone. "I do not wish, nor should I deem it kind or wise, that you should abruptly cease to take any notice of her. Now, for instance, you could limit yourself to an occasional quarter of an hour's chat, say, for example just before meals." "Always to be taken in public," put in Mrs. Hughes. . "Sounds like a dose of medicine," observed Dennis, laughing. "Oh, don't be frivolous," implored his aunt. "Try and talk seriously for once in your life. Levity at such a moment is entirely misplaced." "I am serious—was never more serious in all in, life," answered Dennis, struggling to suppress Ins sense of amusement. "But on the whole I am rather taken with this suggestion of yours, uncle. There are four meals during the day; and it would be much more convenient to lump the quarters of an hour together, and let them run consecutively. You remember, aunt, how when a youngster I always wanted to eat my daily allowance of four pieces of cake off at a go? It's the same with me now. I don't like taking my pleasures piecemeal. So every evening after dinner I will ask Miss Treherne just to come for all hour's saunter with me—alone, of course! " Mr. and Mrs. Hughes exchanged glances of horror. "Really, Dennis," observed the latter. "I never knew anyone so dense as you are. Your uncle and I have been trying to make you understand that it is requisite you should net be CO pronounced in your attentions to Miss Treherne, and now you calmly suggest paying her considerably more pointed attention in the future, than you have in the paste', It is quite incomprehensible to am that you can be! so blind to your duties 1 " Dennis merely smiled, and continued twirling his moustache. Mr. Hughes glanced at his wife in a manner that clearly said: "We have rcted unwisely in thus trying to instruct him in the way he should go." LANCASTIIRE COUNTY COUNCIL—TO THE ELECTORS OF THR NEWTON-IN-MARERFIELD ELECTORAL DIVISION. LLDIE3 AND GENPLEMEN,— On the 3rd day of March, 1893, my secoad term of office as the Representative of the Newtan-in-Makerfield Wiectoral Division on the Lancashire County Council will exulre, and at the request of an influential and representative deputation of Ratepayers I have consented to allow myself ta be nominated for the third time as a candid Re at the ensuing County Council Election. I have therefore pleasure in again placing my services at your disposal. I hive endeavoured to perform the work unier the office during the past sic years to the best of my ability, keeping n vie sr the interests of the constituency and the county generally. If I am again honoured by you with your confidence I will continue to devote my energies to the fulfilment of the duties and responsibilities of the position with every care and a.tention. Soliciting your support. I am, Ladies and Gentlemen, Yours obediently. C. B. F. BORRON. Newton-le-Willows, 17th February, 1893.
